**Haiti**,[^cite_note-10] officially the **Republic of Haiti**,[^cite_note-12][^cite_note-Hayti-18] is a country in the [Caribbean](18956035.md) on the island of [Hispaniola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispaniola) in the [Caribbean Sea](7822.md), east of [Cuba](5042481.md) and [Jamaica](15660.md) and south of [The Bahamas](3451.md). It occupies the western side of the island, which it shares with the [Dominican Republic](8060.md).[^cite_note-Dardik-19][^cite_note-Current_Affairs-20] Haiti is the third largest country in the Caribbean by area, and with an estimated population of 11.4 million, it is the most populous Caribbean country.[^cite_note-UN_WPP-21][^cite_note-UN_WPP_2022-22][^cite_note-23] The capital and largest city is [Port-au-Prince](57391.md).

Haiti was originally inhabited by [Indigenous peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_of_the_Caribbean), known as the [Taíno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ta%C3%ADno).[^cite_note-national-geographic-24] In 1492, [Christopher Columbus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christopher_Columbus) established the first European settlement in the [Americas](29833.md), [La Navidad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Navidad), on its northeastern coast.[^cite_note-Davies1953-25][^cite_note-\:1-26] The island was part of the [Spanish Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Empire) until 1697, when the western portion was [ceded to France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peace_of_Ryswick) and became [Saint-Domingue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint-Domingue), dominated by [sugarcane plantations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sugar_plantations_in_the_Caribbean) worked by enslaved Africans. The 1791–1804 [Haitian Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haitian_Revolution) made Haiti the first [sovereign state](23604120.md) in the Caribbean, the second [republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic) in the Americas, the first country in the Americas to officially abolish slavery, and the only country in history established by a [slave revolt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slave_revolt).[^cite_note-27][^cite_note-28][^cite_note-29] The independent state began as an [empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Empire_of_Haiti), later splitting into two nations before [reunifying](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Haiti_\(1820%E2%80%931849\)) in 1820; a [second empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Empire_of_Haiti) was established under [Faustin Soulouque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faustin_Soulouque) before the [return of the republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Haiti_\(1859%E2%80%931957\)) in 1859. The 19th century also saw political instability, international isolation, a crippling [debt to France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haitian_independence_debt), and failed invasions of the Dominican Republic, including [a costly war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominican_War_of_Independence). [U.S. forces occupied Haiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_occupation_of_Haiti) from 1915 to 1934,[^cite_note-Haite1934-30] followed by the dictatorial rule of the [Duvalier family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duvalier_family) (1957–1986). Following a [coup d'état in 1991](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_Haitian_coup_d'%C3%A9tat), a U.S.-led multinational force [intervened in 1994](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Uphold_Democracy); a [second coup in 2004](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_Haitian_coup_d'%C3%A9tat) was followed by a [United Nations intervention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Stabilisation_Mission_in_Haiti). In the 2010s, [a catastrophic earthquake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_Haiti_earthquake) and [a large-scale cholera outbreak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010s_Haiti_cholera_outbreak) devastated the country.[^cite_note-31]

Historically poor and politically unstable, Haiti has faced [severe economic and political crises](59942434.md), [gang activity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gang_war_in_Haiti) displacing over 1.3 million,[^cite_note-32][^cite_note-33] and the collapse of its government. The presidency has been vacant since the [2021 assassination](68178591.md) of [Jovenel Moïse](48475064.md), while the [National Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Assembly_\(Haiti\)) and the judiciary have been rendered inoperative, leaving the country in virtual anarchy with almost nonexistent state control. One of the world's [least developed countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Least_developed_countries) and with no elected officials remaining, Haiti has been described as a [failed state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Failed_state).[^cite_note-34][^cite_note-35]

Haiti is a founding member of the [United Nations](31769.md), [Organization of American States](22788.md),[^cite_note-36] [Association of Caribbean States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Association_of_Caribbean_States),[^cite_note-37] and the [Organisation internationale de la Francophonie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organisation_internationale_de_la_Francophonie). In addition to [CARICOM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CARICOM), it is a member of the [International Monetary Fund](15251.md),[^cite_note-38] [World Trade Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Trade_Organization),[^cite_note-39] and [Community of Latin American and Caribbean States](26313243.md).

## Etymology

*Haiti* (also earlier *Hayti*)[^cite_note-Hayti-18] comes from the Indigenous [Taíno language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ta%C3%ADno_language) and means "land of high mountains";[^cite_note-40] it was the Native name[^cite_note-44] for the entire island of [Hispaniola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispaniola). The name was restored by Haitian revolutionary [Jean-Jacques Dessalines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Jacques_Dessalines) as the official name of independent [Saint-Domingue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint-Domingue), as a tribute to the Amerindian predecessors.[^cite_note-45] Another theory on the name Haiti is its origin in African tradition; in Fon language, one of the most spoken by the bossales (Haitians born in Africa), [Ayiti-Tomè](http://faculty.webster.edu/corbetre/haiti-archive/msg02162.html) means: "From nowadays this land is our land."

In French, the *[ï](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%8F)* in *Ha**ï**ti* has a diacritical mark, a [diaeresis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diaeresis_\(diacritic\)#French) (used to show that the second vowel is pronounced separately, as in the word *na**ï**ve*), while the *H* is silent.[^cite_note-46] (In English, this rule for the pronunciation is often disregarded, thus the spelling *Haiti* is used.) There are different anglicizations for its pronunciation such as *HIGH-ti*, *high-EE-ti* and *haa-EE-ti*, which are still in use, but *HAY-ti* is the most widespread and best-established.[^cite_note-47] In French, Haiti's nickname is *La Perle des Antilles* ("Pearl of the Antilles") because of both its natural beauty[^cite_note-48] and the amount of wealth it accumulated for the [Kingdom of France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_France).[^cite_note-49] In [Haitian Creole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haitian_Creole), it is spelled and pronounced with a *y* but no *H*: *Ayiti*. In the Haitian community the country has multiple nicknames: Ayiti-Toma (as its origin in Ayiti Tomè), Ayiti-Cheri (Ayiti my Darling), Tè-Desalin (Dessalines' Land) or Lakay (Home).

## History

Main article: [History of Haiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Haiti)

### Pre-Columbian era



The five [caciquedoms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cacique) of Hispaniola at the time of the arrival of Christopher Columbus

Main article: [Chiefdoms of Hispaniola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chiefdoms_of_Hispaniola)

The island of Hispaniola, of which Haiti occupies the western three-eighths,[^cite_note-Dardik-19][^cite_note-Current_Affairs-20] has been inhabited since about 6,000 years ago by Indigenous peoples who are thought to have arrived from Central or northern South America. These [Archaic Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archaic_period_\(North_America\)) people are thought to have been largely [hunter-gatherers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hunter-gatherer). During the 1st millennium BC, the [Arawakan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arawakan_languages)-speaking ancestors of the [Taino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ta%C3%ADno) people began to migrate into the Caribbean. Unlike the Archaic peoples, they practiced the intensive production of pottery and agriculture. The earliest evidence of the ancestors of the Taino people on Hispaniola is the Ostionoid culture, which dates to around 600 AD.[^cite_note-\:132-50]

In Taíno society the largest unit of political organization was led by a *[cacique](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cacique)*, or chief, as the Europeans understood them. At the time of European contact, Hispaniola was divided among five 'caciquedoms': the Magua in the northeast, the Marien in the northwest, the Jaragua in the southwest, the Maguana in the central regions of Cibao, and the Higüey in the southeast.[^cite_note-51][^cite_note-52]

Taíno cultural artifacts include [cave paintings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cave_paintings) in several locations in the country. These have become national symbols of Haiti and tourist attractions. Modern-day [Léogâne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L%C3%A9og%C3%A2ne), started as a French colonial town in the southwest, is beside the former capital of the caciquedom of *[Xaraguá](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xaragu%C3%A1).*[^cite_note-royal-53]

According to the Puerto Rican historian [Cayetano Coll y Toste](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cayetano_Coll_y_Toste), the German explorer [Carl Friedrich Philipp von Martius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carl_Friedrich_Philipp_von_Martius) was the first person to describe the [Indigenous peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_of_the_Caribbean) of Haiti as "Taini" ([Taino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taino)). The archaeologist [L. Antonio Curet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L._Antonio_Curet) has written that Martius described these peoples as "Taini...perhaps by mistaking the qualifier for an ethnonym".[^cite_note-54]

### Colonial era

#### Spanish rule (1492–1625)

Main articles: [Columbian Viceroyalty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Columbian_Viceroyalty), [New Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Spain), and [Captaincy General of Santo Domingo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Captaincy_General_of_Santo_Domingo)



[Artist's impression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artist's_impression) of [Christopher Columbus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christopher_Columbus) landing on [Hispaniola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispaniola), engraving by [Theodor de Bry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theodor_de_Bry)

Navigator [Christopher Columbus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christopher_Columbus) landed in Haiti on 6 December 1492, in an area that he named *[Môle-Saint-Nicolas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C3%B4le-Saint-Nicolas)*,[^cite_note-55] and claimed the island for the [Crown of Castile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_of_Castile). On 25 December his ship the *[Santa María](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Mar%C3%ADa_\(ship\))* ran aground near the present site of [Cap-Haïtien](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cap-Ha%C3%AFtien). Columbus left 39 men on the island, who founded the settlement of [La Navidad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Navidad).[^cite_note-Encylopedia_Britannica_-_Haiti-56] Relations with the native peoples were initially good; however the settlers were later killed by the Taíno.[^cite_note-Bradt10-57]

The sailors carried endemic Eurasian infectious diseases, causing epidemics that killed a large number of native people.[^cite_note-58][^cite_note-59] The first recorded [smallpox epidemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_smallpox) in the Americas erupted on Hispaniola in 1507.[^cite_note-60] Their numbers were further reduced by the harshness of the *_[encomienda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Encomienda)_* system, in which the Spanish forced natives to work in gold mines and plantations.[^cite_note-61][^cite_note-Bradt10-57] The Spanish passed the [Laws of Burgos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laws_of_Burgos) (1512–1513) which forbade the maltreatment of natives, endorsed their conversion to Catholicism,[^cite_note-62] and gave legal framework to **encomiendas*.* The natives were brought to these sites to work in specific plantations or industries.[^cite_note-63]

As the Spanish re-focused their colonization efforts on the greater riches of mainland Central and South America, Hispaniola became reduced largely to a trading and refueling post. As a result [piracy became widespread](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Piracy_in_the_Caribbean), encouraged by European powers hostile to Spain such as France (based on [Île de la Tortue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tortuga_\(Haiti\))) and England.[^cite_note-Bradt10-57] The Spanish largely abandoned the western third of the island, focusing their colonization effort on the eastern two-thirds.[^cite_note-64][^cite_note-Encylopedia_Britannica_-_Haiti-56] The western part of the island was thus gradually settled by French [buccaneers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buccaneer); among them was [Bertrand d'Ogeron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bertrand_d'Ogeron), who succeeded in growing [tobacco](30942.md) and recruited many French colonial families from [Martinique](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martinique) and [Guadeloupe](12343.md).[^cite_note-65] In 1697 [France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_France) and [Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Empire) settled their hostilities on the island by way of the [Treaty of Ryswick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Ryswick) of 1697, which divided Hispaniola between them.[^cite_note-Bradt11-66][^cite_note-Encylopedia_Britannica_-_Haiti-56]

#### French rule (1625–1804)

Main articles: [Saint-Domingue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint-Domingue) and [French West Indies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_West_Indies)

France received the western third and subsequently named it Saint-Domingue, the French equivalent of *[Santo Domingo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Captaincy_General_of_Santo_Domingo)*, the Spanish colony on Hispaniola.[^cite_note-firstcolony-67] The French set about creating sugar and coffee plantations, worked by vast numbers of those enslaved imported from Africa, and Saint-Domingue grew to become their richest colonial possession,[^cite_note-Bradt11-66][^cite_note-Encylopedia_Britannica_-_Haiti-56] generating 40% of France’s foreign trade and doubling the wealth generation of all of England’s colonies, combined.[^cite_note-68]

The French settlers were outnumbered by enslaved persons by almost 10 to 1.[^cite_note-Bradt11-66] According to the 1788 census, Haiti's population consisted of nearly 25,000 Europeans, 22,000 free coloreds and 700,000 Africans in slavery.[^cite_note-69] In the north of the island, those enslaved were able to retain many ties to African cultures, religion and language; these ties were continually being renewed by newly imported Africans. Some West Africans in slavery held on to their traditional [Vodou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haitian_Vodou) beliefs by secretly syncretizing it with Catholicism.[^cite_note-Encylopedia_Britannica_-_Haiti-56]

The French enacted the *[Code Noir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Code_Noir)* ("Black Code"), prepared by [Jean-Baptiste Colbert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Baptiste_Colbert) and ratified by [Louis XIV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_XIV), which established rules on slave treatment and permissible freedoms.[^cite_note-Bradt12-70] Saint-Domingue has been described as one of the most brutally efficient slave colonies; at the end of the 18th century it was supplying two-thirds of Europe's tropical produce while one-third of newly imported Africans died within a few years.[^cite_note-Farmer-LROB-71] Many enslaved persons died from diseases such as [smallpox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smallpox) and [typhoid fever](31596.md).[^cite_note-72] They had low birth rates,[^cite_note-73] and there is evidence that some women aborted fetuses rather than give birth to children within the bonds of slavery.[^cite_note-74] The colony's environment also suffered, as forests were cleared to make way for plantations and the land was overworked so as to extract maximum profit for French plantation owners.[^cite_note-Encylopedia_Britannica_-_Haiti-56]



Saint-Domingue [slave revolt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slave_rebellion) in 1791

As in its [Louisiana colony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_\(New_France\)), the [French colonial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_France) government allowed some rights to [free people of color](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_people_of_color) (*gens de couleur*), the [mixed-race](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mixed-race) descendants of European male colonists and African enslaved females (and later, mixed-race women).[^cite_note-Bradt11-66] Over time, many were released from slavery, and they established a separate social class. White French [Creole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Creole_peoples) fathers frequently sent their mixed-race sons to France for their education. Some men of color were admitted into the military. More of the free people of color lived in the south of the island, near Port-au-Prince, and many intermarried within their community.[^cite_note-Bradt11-66] They frequently worked as artisans and tradesmen and began to own some property, including enslaved persons of their own.[^cite_note-Encylopedia_Britannica_-_Haiti-56][^cite_note-Bradt11-66] The free people of color petitioned the colonial government to expand their rights.[^cite_note-Bradt11-66]

The brutality of slave life led many people in bondage to escape to mountainous regions, where they set up their own autonomous communities and became known as [maroons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maroons).[^cite_note-Encylopedia_Britannica_-_Haiti-56] One maroon leader, [François Mackandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fran%C3%A7ois_Mackandal), led a rebellion in the 1750s; however, he was later captured and executed by the French.[^cite_note-Bradt11-66]

#### Haitian Revolution (1791–1804)

Main article: [Haitian Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haitian_Revolution)



[General](148537.md) Toussaint Louverture

Inspired by the [French Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Revolution) of 1789 and principles of the [rights of man](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Declaration_of_the_Rights_of_Man_and_of_the_Citizen), the French settlers and free people of color pressed for greater political freedom and more civil rights.[^cite_note-Bradt12-70] Tensions between these two groups led to conflict, and a militia of free-coloreds was set up in 1790 by [Vincent Ogé](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vincent_Og%C3%A9), resulting in his capture, torture and execution.[^cite_note-Encylopedia_Britannica_-_Haiti-56] Sensing an opportunity, in August 1791 the first slave armies were established in northern Haiti under the leadership of [Toussaint Louverture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toussaint_Louverture) inspired by the Vodou *houngan* (priest) Boukman, and backed by the Spanish in Santo Domingo – soon a full-blown slave rebellion had broken out across the entire colony.[^cite_note-Encylopedia_Britannica_-_Haiti-56]

In 1792 , the [French First Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_First_Republic) sent three commissioners with troops to re-establish control. To build an alliance with the *[gens de couleur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gens_de_couleur)* and enslaved persons, commissioners [Léger-Félicité Sonthonax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L%C3%A9ger-F%C3%A9licit%C3%A9_Sonthonax) and [Étienne Polverel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89tienne_Polverel) abolished slavery in the colony.[^cite_note-Bradt12-70] Six months later, the [National Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Convention) endorsed [abolition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abolition_of_slavery_timeline) and extended it to all the French colonies.[^cite_note-75]

The United States oscillated between supporting or not supporting Louverture and the emerging country of Haiti, depending on who was President of the US. [George Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Washington), who was a slave holder and isolationist, kept the United States neutral, although private US citizens at times provided aid to French [planters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planter_\(plantation_owner\)) trying to put down the revolt. [John Adams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Adams), a vocal opponent of both slavery and Revolutionary France, fully supported the slave revolt by providing diplomatic recognition, financial support, munitions and warships (including the [USS *Constitution*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Constitution)) beginning in 1798. This support ended in 1801 when [Thomas Jefferson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Jefferson), another [slave-holding president](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Jefferson_and_slavery), took office and recalled the US Navy.[^cite_note-76][^cite_note-77][^cite_note-78]

With slavery abolished, Louverture pledged allegiance to France, and he fought off the British and Spanish forces who had taken advantage of the situation and had invaded Saint-Domingue.[^cite_note-Latin_America's_Wars:_Volume_1-79][^cite_note-80] The Spanish were later forced to cede their part of the island to France under the terms of the [Peace of Basel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peace_of_Basel) in 1795, uniting the island under one government. However, an insurgency against French rule broke out in the east, and in the west there was fighting between Louverture's forces and the free people of color led by [André Rigaud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andr%C3%A9_Rigaud) in the [War of the Knives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_of_the_Knives) (1799–1800).[^cite_note-81][^cite_note-82] The United States' support for the blacks in the war contributed to their victory over the mulattoes.[^cite_note-YPT-83] More than 25,000 whites and free blacks left the island as refugees.[^cite_note-84]



Battle between [Polish troops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_Legions_\(Napoleonic_period\)) in French service and the [Haitian rebels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haitian_Revolution). The majority of Polish soldiers eventually deserted the French army and fought alongside the Haitians.

After Louverture created a separatist constitution and proclaimed himself governor-general for life, [Napoléon Bonaparte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napol%C3%A9on_Bonaparte) in 1802 [sent an expedition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint-Domingue_expedition) of 20,000 soldiers and as many sailors[^cite_note-85] under the command of his brother-in-law [Charles Leclerc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Leclerc_\(general,_born_1772\)) to reassert French control. The French achieved some victories, but within a few months most of their army had died from [yellow fever](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yellow_fever).[^cite_note-86] Ultimately more than 50,000 French troops died in an attempt to retake the colony, including 18 generals.[^cite_note-87] The French managed to capture Louverture, transporting him to France for trial. He was imprisoned at [Fort de Joux](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_de_Joux), where he died in 1803 of exposure and possibly [tuberculosis](30653.md).[^cite_note-Farmer-LROB-71][^cite_note-Bradt13-88]



Haitians hanging French soldiers

The enslaved persons, along with free *gens de couleur* and allies, continued their fight for independence, led by generals [Jean-Jacques Dessalines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Jacques_Dessalines), [Alexandre Pétion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandre_P%C3%A9tion) and [Henry Christophe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Christophe).[^cite_note-Bradt13-88] The rebels finally managed to decisively defeat the French troops at the [Battle of Vertières](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Verti%C3%A8res) on 18 November 1803, establishing the only state to successfully gain independence through a slave revolt.[^cite_note-89] Under the overall command of Dessalines, the Haitian armies avoided open battle and instead conducted a successful [guerrilla](12720.md) campaign against the Napoleonic forces, working with diseases such as yellow fever to reduce the numbers of French soldiers.[^cite_note-90] Later that year France withdrew its remaining 7,000 troops from the island, and Napoleon gave up his idea of re-establishing a North American empire, selling [Louisiana (New France)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_\(New_France\)) to the United States in the [Louisiana Purchase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_Purchase).[^cite_note-Bradt13-88] On 1 January 1804, the leaders of the Haitian Revolution declared their achieved independence from France.[^cite_note-r015-91]

Throughout the revolution, an estimated 20,000 French troops succumbed to yellow fever, while another 37,000 were killed in action,[^cite_note-92] exceeding the total French soldiers killed in action across various 19th-century colonial campaigns in Algeria, Mexico, Indochina, Tunisia, and West Africa, which resulted in approximately 10,000 French soldiers killed in action combined.[^cite_note-93] The British sustained 45,000 dead. Additionally, 200,000 Haitians died.[^cite_note-Scheina-94] In the process, Dessalines became arguably the most successful military commander in the struggle against Napoleonic France.[^cite_note-95]

### Independent Haiti

#### First Empire (1804–1806)

Main articles: [First Empire of Haiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Empire_of_Haiti) and [1804 Haitian massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1804_Haitian_massacre)



Pétion and Dessalines swearing allegiance to each other before God; painting by [Guillon-Lethière](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guillaume_Guillon-Lethi%C3%A8re)

The independence of Saint-Domingue was proclaimed under the native name 'Haiti' by Dessalines on 1 January 1804 in [Gonaïves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gona%C3%AFves),[^cite_note-Webster_University-96][^cite_note-Bradt209-97] and he was proclaimed "Emperor for Life" as Emperor Jacques I by his troops.[^cite_note-98] Dessalines at first offered protection to the white planters and others.[^cite_note-99] However, once in power, he ordered the [1804 Haitian massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1804_Haitian_massacre) of nearly all the remaining whites; between January and April 1804, 3,000 to 5,000 whites were killed, including those who had been friendly and sympathetic to the black population.[^cite_note-Davies2008-100] Only [three categories of white people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1804_Haiti_massacre#Aftermath) were selected out as exceptions and spared: [Polish soldiers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_Haitians), some of whom had deserted from the French army and fought alongside the Haitian rebels; a small group of [German colonists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Haitians) invited to the north-west region; and a group of medical doctors and professionals.[^cite_note-101] Reportedly, people with connections to officers in the Haitian army were also spared, as well as the women who agreed to marry non-white men.[^cite_note-102]

Fearful of the potential impact the slave rebellion could have in the [slave states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slave_states_and_free_states), U.S. President Jefferson refused to recognize the new republic. The Southern politicians who were a powerful voting bloc in the American Congress prevented U.S. recognition for decades until they withdrew in 1861 to form the [Confederacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confederate_States_of_America).[^cite_note-103] The revolution led to a wave of emigration.[^cite_note-104] In 1809, 9,000 refugees from Saint-Domingue, both white planters and people of color, settled *en masse* in [New Orleans](53842.md), doubling the city's population,[^cite_note-105] having been expelled from their initial refuge in Cuba by Spanish authorities.[^cite_note-106]

The plantation system was re-established in Haiti, albeit for wages; however, many Haitians were marginalized and resented the heavy-handed manner in which this was enforced in the new nation's politics.[^cite_note-Bradt13-88] The rebel movement splintered, and Dessalines was assassinated by rivals on 17 October 1806.[^cite_note-Haiti-107][^cite_note-Bradt13-88]

#### State of Haiti, Kingdom of Haiti and the Republic (1806–1820)

Main articles: [State of Haiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_of_Haiti), [Kingdom of Haiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Haiti), and [Republic of Haiti (1806–1820)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Haiti_\(1806%E2%80%931820\))



[Citadelle Laferrière](2303791.md), built 1805–1822, is the largest [fortress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fortress) in the Americas, and is considered locally to be an [eighth wonder of the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eighth_wonder_of_the_world).

[^cite_note-Reading_Eagle-108]

After Dessalines' death, Haiti became split into two, with the [Kingdom of Haiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Haiti) in the north directed by [Henri Christophe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henri_Christophe), later declaring himself Henri I, and a republic in the south centered on Port-au-Prince, directed by [Alexandre Pétion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandre_P%C3%A9tion), an *homme de couleur*.[^cite_note-109][^cite_note-110][^cite_note-111][^cite_note-112] Pétion's republic was less absolutist, and he initiated a series of land reforms which benefited the peasant class.[^cite_note-Bradt13-88] Pétion also gave military and financial assistance to the revolutionary leader [Simón Bolívar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sim%C3%B3n_Bol%C3%ADvar), which were critical in enabling him to liberate the [Viceroyalty of New Granada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viceroyalty_of_New_Granada).[^cite_note-113] Meanwhile, the French, who had managed to maintain a precarious control of eastern Hispaniola, were [defeated by insurgents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_reconquest_of_Santo_Domingo) led by [Juan Sánchez Ramírez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_S%C3%A1nchez_Ram%C3%ADrez), with the area returning to Spanish rule in 1809 following the [Battle of Palo Hincado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Palo_Hincado).[^cite_note-RECONQUEST-114]

#### Unification of Hispaniola (1821–1844)

Main articles: [Republic of Haiti (1820–1849)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Haiti_\(1820%E2%80%931849\)) and [Haitian occupation of Santo Domingo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haitian_occupation_of_Santo_Domingo)



[Jean-Pierre Boyer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Pierre_Boyer) (r. 1818–1843)

Beginning in 1821, President [Jean-Pierre Boyer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Pierre_Boyer), also an *homme de couleur* and successor to Pétion, reunified the island following the suicide of Christophe.[^cite_note-Encylopedia_Britannica_-_Haiti-56][^cite_note-115] After [Santo Domingo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Spanish_Haiti) declared its independence from Spain on 30 November 1821, Boyer invaded, seeking to unite the entire island by force and to end slavery in Santo Domingo.[^cite_note-116] Struggling to revive the agricultural economy to produce commodity crops, Boyer passed the Code Rural, which denied peasant laborers the right to leave the land, enter the towns, or start farms or shops of their own, causing much resentment as most peasants wished to have their own farms rather than work on plantations.[^cite_note-117][^cite_note-118] Boyer's administration also reserved the highest posts in the administrative bureaucracy for Haitian mulattoes, contributing to resentment among Dominicans.[^cite_note-119]

Starting in 1824, more than 6,000 [African Americans](2154.md) migrated to Haiti, with transportation paid by an American philanthropic group similar in function to the [American Colonization Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Colonization_Society) and its efforts in [Liberia](17791.md).[^cite_note-120][^cite_note-auto7-121] Many found the conditions too harsh and returned to the United States.[^cite_note-auto7-121]

In July 1825, King [Charles X of France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_X_of_France), during a period of restoration of the French monarchy, sent a fleet to reconquer Haiti. Under pressure, Boyer agreed to a treaty by which France formally recognized the independence of the state in exchange for [a payment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haiti_indemnity_controversy) of 150 million [francs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francs), or \$560 million in today's dollars.[^cite_note-Encylopedia_Britannica_-_Haiti-56][^cite_note-Nagourney-122] By an order of 17 April 1826, the King of France renounced his rights of sovereignty and formally recognized the independence of Haiti.[^cite_note-FrenchEmbassy-123][^cite_note-124][^cite_note-125] The enforced payments to France hampered Haiti's economic growth for years, exacerbated by the fact that many [Western](21208200.md) states continued to refuse formal diplomatic recognition to Haiti; Britain recognized Haitian independence in 1833, and the United States not until 1862.[^cite_note-Encylopedia_Britannica_-_Haiti-56] Haiti borrowed heavily from Western banks at extremely high interest rates to repay the debt, an amount estimated to be between \$21 billion and \$115 billion in lost economic growth over time, or as much as eight times the size of Haiti's economy in 2020.[^cite_note-Nagourney-122] Although the amount of the reparations was reduced to \$90 million in 1838, by 1900 80% of Haiti's government spending was debt repayment, and the country did not finish repaying it until 1947.[^cite_note-126][^cite_note-Bradt13-88]

#### Loss of the eastern portion of the island

Main article: [Dominican War of Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominican_War_of_Independence)



Charles Rivière-Hérard (r. 1843–1844)

After losing the support of Haiti's elite, Boyer was ousted in 1843, with [Charles Rivière-Hérard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Rivi%C3%A8re-H%C3%A9rard) replacing him as president.[^cite_note-Encylopedia_Britannica_-_Haiti-56] Nationalist Dominican forces in eastern Hispaniola seized control of Santo Domingo on 27 February 1844.[^cite_note-Encylopedia_Britannica_-_Haiti-56] The Haitian forces, unprepared for a significant uprising, capitulated to the rebels, effectively ending Haitian rule of eastern Hispaniola. In March, Rivière-Hérard attempted to reimpose his authority, but the [Dominicans](8060.md) inflicted heavy losses.[^cite_note-Bethell-127] Rivière-Hérard was removed from office by the mulatto hierarchy and replaced with the aged general [Philippe Guerrier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippe_Guerrier), who assumed the presidency on 3 May 1844.

Guerrier died in April 1845 and was succeeded by General [Jean-Louis Pierrot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Louis_Pierrot).[^cite_note-leger197-128] Pierrot's most pressing duty was to check the incursions of the Dominicans, who were harassing the Haitian troops.[^cite_note-leger197-128] Dominican gunboats were also making depredations on Haiti's coasts.[^cite_note-leger197-128] President Pierrot decided to open a campaign against the Dominicans, whom he considered merely as insurgents; however, the Haitian offensive of 1845 was stopped on the frontier.[^cite_note-Bethell-127]

On 1 January 1846, Pierrot announced a fresh campaign to reimpose Haitian suzerainty over eastern Hispaniola, but his officers and men greeted this fresh summons with contempt.[^cite_note-Bethell-127] Thus, a month later – February 1846 – when Pierrot ordered his troops to march against the Dominicans, the Haitian army mutinied, and its soldiers proclaimed his overthrow as president of the republic.[^cite_note-Bethell-127] With the war against the Dominicans having become very unpopular in Haiti, it was beyond the power of the new president, General [Jean-Baptiste Riché](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Baptiste_Rich%C3%A9), to stage another invasion.[^cite_note-Bethell-127]

#### Second Empire (1849–1859)

Main article: [Second Empire of Haiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Empire_of_Haiti)



Faustin I, from *[The Illustrated London News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Illustrated_London_News)*, 16 February 1856

On 27 February 1847, Riché died after only a year in power and was replaced by an obscure officer, General [Faustin Soulouque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faustin_Soulouque).[^cite_note-Encylopedia_Britannica_-_Haiti-56] During the first two years of Soulouque's administration the conspiracies and opposition he faced in retaining power were so manifold that the Dominicans were given a further breathing space in which to consolidate their independence.[^cite_note-Bethell-127] But, when in 1848 France finally recognized the Dominican Republic as a free and independent state and provisionally signed a treaty of peace, friendship, commerce and navigation, Haiti immediately protested, claiming the treaty was an attack upon their own security.[^cite_note-Bethell-127] Soulouque decided to invade the new Republic before the French Government could ratify the treaty.[^cite_note-Bethell-127]

On 21 March 1849, Haitian soldiers attacked the Dominican garrison at [Las Matas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Las_Matas_de_Farf%C3%A1n), which offered almost no resistance before abandoning its weapons. Soulouque pressed on, capturing [San Juan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Juan_Province_\(Dominican_Republic\)), leaving only the town of [Azua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azua,_Dominican_Republic) as the remaining Dominican stronghold between the Haitian army and the capital. On 6 April, Azua fell to the 18,000-strong Haitian army, with a 5,000-man Dominican counterattack failing to oust them.[^cite_note-Latin_America's_Wars:_Volume_1-79] According to the Haitian account, the way to [Santo Domingo](57638.md) was now clear, but the news of discontent existing at Port-au-Prince, which reached Soulouque, halted his advance and caused him to return with the army to his capital.[^cite_note-Jacques-129]

Emboldened by the sudden retreat of the Haitian army, the Dominicans counter-attacked; their flotilla went as far as [Dame-Marie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dame-Marie,_Grand'Anse) on the [Tiburon Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tiburon_Peninsula), which they plundered and set on fire.[^cite_note-Jacques-129] After another Haitian campaign in 1855, [Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Kingdom_of_Great_Britain_and_Ireland) and [France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_French_Empire) intervened and obtained an [armistice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armistice) on behalf of the Dominicans, who declared independence as the Dominican Republic.[^cite_note-Jacques-129]

The sufferings endured by the soldiers during the campaign of 1855, and the losses and sacrifices inflicted on the country without yielding any compensation or any practical results provoked great discontent.[^cite_note-Jacques-129] In 1858 a revolution began, led by General [Fabre Geffrard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fabre_Geffrard). In December of that year, Geffrard defeated the army and seized control of most of the country.[^cite_note-Encylopedia_Britannica_-_Haiti-56] As a result, Soulouque abdicated his throne on 15 January 1859 and was taken into exile, and Geffrard succeeded him as president.[^cite_note-130]

#### Late 19th century–early 20th century



German captain August Carl Thiele delivering the German ultimatum during the 1897 [Lüders affair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L%C3%BCders_affair)

The period following Soulouque's overthrow down to the turn of the century was a turbulent one for Haiti, with repeated bouts of political instability. Geffrard was overthrown in a coup in 1867,[^cite_note-131] as was his successor, [Sylvain Salnave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sylvain_Salnave), in 1869.[^cite_note-132] Under the Presidency of [Michel Domingue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michel_Domingue) (1874–1876) relations with the Dominican Republic were dramatically improved by the signing of a treaty, in which both parties acknowledged the independence of the other. Some modernisation of the economy and infrastructure also occurred in this period, especially under the presidencies of [Lysius Salomon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lysius_Salomon) (1879–1888) and [Florvil Hyppolite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florvil_Hyppolite) (1889–1896).[^cite_note-133]

Haiti's relations with outside powers were often strained. In 1889 the United States attempted to [force Haiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C3%B4le_Saint-Nicolas_affair) to permit the building of a naval base at [Môle Saint-Nicolas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C3%B4le_Saint-Nicolas), which was firmly resisted by President Hyppolite.[^cite_note-leger245-134] In 1892 the German government supported suppression of the reform movement of [Anténor Firmin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ant%C3%A9nor_Firmin), and in 1897 the Germans used [gunboat diplomacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gunboat_diplomacy) to intimidate and then humiliate the Haitian government of President [Tirésias Simon Sam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tir%C3%A9sias_Simon_Sam) (1896–1902) during the [Lüders affair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L%C3%BCders_affair).[^cite_note-135]

In the first decades of the 20th century, Haiti experienced great political instability and was heavily in debt to France, Germany and the United States. A series of short lived presidencies came and went: [Pierre Nord Alexis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pierre_Nord_Alexis) was forced from power in 1908,[^cite_note-136][^cite_note-137] as was his successor [François C. Antoine Simon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fran%C3%A7ois_C._Antoine_Simon) in 1911;[^cite_note-138] [Cincinnatus Leconte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cincinnatus_Leconte) (1911–1912) was killed in a (possibly deliberate) explosion at the National Palace;[^cite_note-139] [Michel Oreste](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michel_Oreste) (1913–1914) was ousted in a coup, as was his successor [Oreste Zamor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oreste_Zamor) in 1914.[^cite_note-140]

#### United States occupation (1915–1934)

Main article: [United States occupation of Haiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_occupation_of_Haiti)

See also: [Slavery in Haiti § Unfree labor during US occupation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery_in_Haiti#Unfree_labor_during_US_occupation)



U.S. Marines and guide in search of Haitian [Cacos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cacos_\(military_group\)) fighters against the [U.S. occupation of Haiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_occupation_of_Haiti), c. 1919

Germany increased its influence in Haiti in this period, with a small community of German settlers wielding disproportionate influence in Haiti's economy.[^cite_note-state-141][^cite_note-Bradt15-142] The German influence prompted anxieties in the United States, who had also invested heavily in the country and who defended their right to oppose foreign interference in the Americas under the [Monroe Doctrine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monroe_Doctrine).[^cite_note-Encylopedia_Britannica_-_Haiti-56][^cite_note-Bradt15-142] In December 1914 the Americans removed \$500,000 from the Haitian National Bank, but rather than seize it to help pay the debt, it was removed for safe-keeping in New York, thus giving the United States control of the bank and preventing other powers from doing so. This gave a stable financial base on which to build the economy and to enable the debt to be repaid.[^cite_note-143]

In 1915, Haiti President [Vilbrun Guillaume Sam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vilbrun_Guillaume_Sam) sought to strengthen his tenuous rule by a mass execution of 167 political prisoners. Outrage at the killings led to riots, and Sam was captured and killed by a lynch mob.[^cite_note-Bradt15-142][^cite_note-144] Fearing possible foreign intervention or the emergence of a government led by the anti-American Haitian politician [Rosalvo Bobo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rosalvo_Bobo), President [Woodrow Wilson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woodrow_Wilson) sent U.S. Marines into Haiti in July 1915. The [USS *Washington*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Washington_\(ACR-11\)), under Rear Admiral [Caperton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Banks_Caperton), arrived in Port-au-Prince in an attempt to restore order and protect U.S. interests. Within days, the Marines had taken control of the capital city and its banks and customs house. The Marines declared martial law and severely censored the press. Within weeks, pro-U.S. Haitian President [Philippe Sudré Dartiguenave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippe_Sudr%C3%A9_Dartiguenave) was installed, and a new constitution was written that was favorable to the interests of the United States. The constitution (written by future US President [Franklin D. Roosevelt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franklin_D._Roosevelt)) included a clause that allowed, for the first time, foreign ownership of land in Haiti, which was bitterly opposed by the Haitian legislature and citizenry.[^cite_note-Bradt15-142][^cite_note-145]



The body of caco leader [Charlemagne Péralte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charlemagne_P%C3%A9ralte) on display after his execution by US forces; the image was counterproductive, with the resemblance to the [deposition of Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Descent_from_the_Cross) gaining Péralte the status of national martyr.

The occupation improved some of Haiti's infrastructure and centralized power in Port-au-Prince.[^cite_note-Bradt15-142] 1700 km of roads were made usable, 189 bridges were built, many irrigation canals were rehabilitated, hospitals, schools, and public buildings were constructed, and drinking water was brought to the main cities. Agricultural education was organized, with a central school of agriculture and 69 farms in the country.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeinl1996454–455-146] However, many infrastructure projects were built using the [corvée](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corv%C3%A9e) system that allowed the government/occupying forces to take people from their homes and farms, at gunpoint if necessary, to build infrastructure by force, a process that was deeply resented by ordinary Haitians.[^cite_note-147][^cite_note-Bradt15-142] [Sisal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sisal) was also introduced to Haiti, and sugarcane and [cotton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cotton) became significant exports, boosting prosperity.[^cite_note-Henl,_pp._454–455-148] Haitian traditionalists based in rural areas were highly resistant to U.S.-backed changes, while the urban elites, typically mixed-race, welcomed the growing economy but wanted more political control.[^cite_note-Encylopedia_Britannica_-_Haiti-56] Together they helped secure an end to the occupation in 1934, under the Presidency of [Sténio Vincent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St%C3%A9nio_Vincent).[^cite_note-Encylopedia_Britannica_-_Haiti-56][^cite_note-149] The debts were still outstanding, though less due to increased prosperity, and the U.S. financial advisor-general receiver handled the budget until 1941.[^cite_note-150][^cite_note-Encylopedia_Britannica_-_Haiti-56]

The U.S. Marines were instilled with a special brand of paternalism towards Haitians "expressed in the metaphor of a father's relationship with his children."[^cite_note-151] Armed opposition to the US presence was led by the [Cacos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cacos_\(military_group\)) under the command of [Charlemagne Péralte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charlemagne_P%C3%A9ralte); his capture and execution in 1919 earned him the status of a national martyr.[^cite_note-152][^cite_note-Encylopedia_Britannica_-_Haiti-56][^cite_note-Bradt15-142] During US Senate hearings in 1921, the commandant of the Marine Corps reported that in the 20 months of active unrest, 2,250 Haitians had been killed. However, in a report to the Secretary of the Navy, he reported the death toll as 3,250.[^cite_note-153] Haitian historians have claimed the true number was much higher, but this is not supported by most historians outside Haiti.[^cite_note-154]

#### Post-occupation era (1934–1957)

After U.S. forces left in 1934, Dominican dictator [Rafael Trujillo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rafael_Trujillo) used [anti-Haitian sentiment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Haitian_sentiment_in_the_Dominican_Republic) as a nationalist tool. In 1937, in an event that became known as the [Parsley Massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parsley_massacre), he ordered his army to kill Haitians living on the Dominican side of the border.[^cite_note-Farmer180-155][^cite_note-wucker-156] Few bullets were used; instead, 20,000–30,000 Haitians were bludgeoned and bayoneted, then herded into the sea, where sharks finished what Trujillo had begun.[^cite_note-157] The indiscriminate massacre occurred over a period of five days and involved hundreds of Dominican troops.

Haitian President [Sténio Vincent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St%C3%A9nio_Vincent), under US pressure owing to his increasingly dictatorial actions, resigned in 1941 and was replaced by [Élie Lescot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89lie_Lescot).[^cite_note-158] In December 1941, during the [Second World War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_World_War), Lescot declared war on Japan, Germany, Italy, Bulgaria, Hungary, and Romania.[^cite_note-159] Out of these six [Axis powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Axis_powers), only Romania reciprocated.[^cite_note-160] On 27 September 1945,[^cite_note-161] Haiti became a [founding member](31969.md) of the [United Nations](31769.md) (the successor to the [League of Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Member_states_of_the_League_of_Nations), of which Haiti was also a founding member).[^cite_note-162][^cite_note-163]

In 1946 Lescot was overthrown by the military, with [Dumarsais Estimé](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dumarsais_Estim%C3%A9) becoming president.[^cite_note-Encylopedia_Britannica_-_Haiti-56] Estimé sought to improve the economy and education, and to boost the role of black Haitians; however, as he sought to consolidate his rule he too was overthrown in a coup led by [Paul Magloire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Magloire), who replaced him in 1950.[^cite_note-Encylopedia_Britannica_-_Haiti-56][^cite_note-164] Firmly anti-Communist, he was supported by the United States; with greater political stability tourists started to visit Haiti.[^cite_note-guardiantourism-165] The waterfront area of Port-au-Prince was redeveloped to allow cruise ship passengers to walk to cultural attractions.

#### Duvalier dynasty (1957–1986)

Main article: [Duvalier dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duvalier_dynasty)



[François Duvalier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fran%C3%A7ois_Duvalier) ("Papa Doc") after being elected in 1957

In 1956–57, Haiti underwent severe political turmoil; Magloire was forced to resign and leave the country in 1956, and he was followed by four short-lived presidencies.[^cite_note-Encylopedia_Britannica_-_Haiti-56] In the [September 1957 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1957_Haitian_general_election), [François Duvalier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fran%C3%A7ois_Duvalier) was elected president. Known as 'Papa Doc' and initially popular, Duvalier remained president until his death in 1971.[^cite_note-Bradt17-166] He advanced black interests in the public sector, where over time people of color had predominated as the educated urban elite.[^cite_note-Encylopedia_Britannica_-_Haiti-56][^cite_note-bryan-167] Not trusting the army despite his frequent purges of officers deemed disloyal, Duvalier created a private militia known as *[Tontons Macoutes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tonton_Macoute)* ("Bogeymen"), which maintained order by terrorizing the populace and political opponents.[^cite_note-Bradt17-166][^cite_note-168] In 1964, Duvalier proclaimed himself 'President for Life'; [an uprising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J%C3%A9r%C3%A9mie_Vespers) against his rule that year in [Jérémie](1714154.md) was violently suppressed, with the ringleaders publicly executed and hundreds of [mixed-raced citizens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mulatto_Haitians) in the town killed.[^cite_note-Bradt17-166] The bulk of the educated and professional class began leaving the country, and corruption became widespread.[^cite_note-Encylopedia_Britannica_-_Haiti-56][^cite_note-Bradt17-166] Duvalier sought to create a personality cult, identifying himself with [Baron Samedi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baron_Samedi), one of the [loa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loa) (or *lwa*), or spirits, of [Haitian Vodou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haitian_Vodou). Despite the well-publicized abuses under his rule, Duvalier's firm [anti-communist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-communist) stance earned him the support of the United States, who furnished the country with aid.[^cite_note-Bradt17-166][^cite_note-Štraus-169]



[Jean-Claude Duvalier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Claude_Duvalier) ("Baby Doc") in 2011

In 1971 Duvalier died, and he was succeeded by his son [Jean-Claude Duvalier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Claude_Duvalier), nicknamed 'Baby Doc', who ruled until 1986.[^cite_note-170][^cite_note-Bradt17-166] He largely continued his father's policies, though he curbed some of the worst excesses in order to court international respectability.[^cite_note-Encylopedia_Britannica_-_Haiti-56] Tourism, which had nosedived in Papa Doc's time, again became a growing industry.[^cite_note-Clammer,_Paul-171] However, as the economy continued to decline, Baby Doc's grip on power began to weaken. Haiti's pig population was slaughtered following an outbreak of [swine fever](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swine_fever) in the late 1970s, causing hardship to rural communities who used them as an investment.[^cite_note-Encylopedia_Britannica_-_Haiti-56][^cite_note-172] The opposition became more vocal, bolstered by a visit to the country by [Pope John Paul II](23805.md) in 1983, who publicly lambasted the president.[^cite_note-173] Demonstrations occurred in [Gonaïves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gona%C3%AFves) in 1985 which then spread across the country; under pressure from the United States, Duvalier left the country in February 1986.[^cite_note-Trouillot-174]

In total, roughly 40,000 to 60,000 Haitians are estimated to have been killed during the reign of the Duvaliers.[^cite_note-175] Through the use of repression and executions, many educated Haitians had fled or went into exile, leaving the country with a massive [brain drain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brain_drain) problem from which it has yet to recover.[^cite_note-176]

#### Post-Duvalier era (1986–2004)



[Jean-Bertrand Aristide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Bertrand_Aristide) returns to Haiti following the [U.S.-led invasion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Uphold_Democracy) in 1994 designed to remove the [regime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raoul_C%C3%A9dras) installed by the [1991 Haitian coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_Haitian_coup_d'%C3%A9tat)

Following Duvalier's departure, army leader General [Henri Namphy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henri_Namphy) headed the [National Governing Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Council_of_Government_\(Haiti\)).[^cite_note-Encylopedia_Britannica_-_Haiti-56] [Elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1987_Haitian_general_election) scheduled for November 1987 were aborted after dozens of inhabitants were shot in the capital by soldiers and *Tontons Macoutes*.[^cite_note-Whitney-177][^cite_note-Encylopedia_Britannica_-_Haiti-56] Fraudulent [elections followed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1988_Haitian_general_election) in 1988, in which only 4% of the citizenry voted.[^cite_note-JC-178][^cite_note-Encylopedia_Britannica_-_Haiti-56] The newly elected president, [Leslie Manigat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leslie_Manigat), was then overthrown some months later in the [June 1988 Haitian coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/June_1988_Haitian_coup_d'%C3%A9tat).[^cite_note-Encylopedia_Britannica_-_Haiti-56][^cite_note-IACHR-179]

Another [coup followed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/September_1988_Haitian_coup_d'%C3%A9tat) in September 1988, after the [St. Jean Bosco massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Jean_Bosco_massacre) in which approximately 13 to 50 people were killed while attending a mass led by prominent government critic and Catholic priest [Jean-Bertrand Aristide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Bertrand_Aristide).[^cite_note-IACHR-179][^cite_note-HRW2-180] General [Prosper Avril](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prosper_Avril) subsequently led a military regime until March 1990.[^cite_note-Encylopedia_Britannica_-_Haiti-56][^cite_note-Rohter-181][^cite_note-182] Avril transferred power to the army chief of staff General [Hérard Abraham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H%C3%A9rard_Abraham) on 10 March. Abraham gave up power three days later, becoming the only military leader in Haiti during the 20th century to voluntarily give up power. Abraham later helped to secure the [1990–91 Haitian general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1990%E2%80%9391_Haitian_general_election),[^cite_note-183] in which Aristide was elected president. However, his ambitious reformist agenda worried the elites, and in September 1991 he was overthrown by the military, led by [Raoul Cédras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raoul_C%C3%A9dras), in the [1991 Haitian coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_Haitian_coup_d'%C3%A9tat).[^cite_note-Encylopedia_Britannica_-_Haiti-56][^cite_note-184] Amidst the continuing turmoil many Haitians attempted to flee the country.[^cite_note-Bradt17-166][^cite_note-Encylopedia_Britannica_-_Haiti-56]

In September 1994, the United States negotiated the departure of Haiti's military leaders and the peaceful entry of 20,000 US troops under [Operation Uphold Democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Uphold_Democracy).[^cite_note-Bradt17-166] This enabled the restoration of the democratically-elected Aristide as president, who returned to Haiti in October to complete his term.[^cite_note-CCHaiti-185][^cite_note-186] As part of the deal Aristide had to implement free market reforms in an attempt to improve the Haitian economy, with mixed results.[^cite_note-Bell-187][^cite_note-Encylopedia_Britannica_-_Haiti-56] In November 1994, [Hurricane Gordon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurricane_Gordon) brushed Haiti, dumping heavy rain and creating flash flooding that triggered mudslides. Gordon killed an estimated 1,122 to 2,200 people.[^cite_note-188][^cite_note-189]

The [1995 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1995_Haitian_general_election) was won by [René Préval](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ren%C3%A9_Pr%C3%A9val), gaining 88% of the popular vote, albeit on a low turnout.[^cite_note-190][^cite_note-191][^cite_note-Encylopedia_Britannica_-_Haiti-56] Aristide subsequently formed his own party, [Fanmi Lavalas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fanmi_Lavalas), and political deadlock ensued; the [November 2000 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haitian_presidential_election,_2000) returned Aristide to the presidency with 92% of the vote.[^cite_note-192] The election had been boycotted by the opposition, then organized into the [Convergence Démocratique](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convergence_D%C3%A9mocratique), over a dispute in the [May legislative elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000_Haitian_parliamentary_election). In subsequent years, there was increasing violence between rival political factions and [human rights abuses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_abuse).[^cite_note-Bradt19-193][^cite_note-bussandgardner-194] Aristide spent years negotiating with the Convergence Démocratique on new elections, but the Convergence's inability to develop a sufficient electoral base made elections unattractive.

In 2004, [an anti-Aristide revolt began](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_Haitian_coup_d'%C3%A9tat) in northern Haiti. The rebellion eventually reached the capital, and Aristide was forced into exile.[^cite_note-Bradt19-193][^cite_note-Encylopedia_Britannica_-_Haiti-56] The nature of the events are disputed; some, including Aristide and his bodyguard Franz Gabriel, stated that he was the victim of a "new coup d'état or modern kidnapping" by U.S. forces.[^cite_note-Bradt19-193][^cite_note-195][^cite_note-196] These charges were denied by the US government.[^cite_note-197][^cite_note-Bradt19-193] As political violence and crime continued to grow, a [United Nations Stabilisation Mission (MINUSTAH)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Stabilisation_Mission_in_Haiti) was brought in to maintain order.[^cite_note-Bradt20-198] However, MINUSTAH proved controversial, since their periodically heavy-handed approach to maintaining law and order and several instances of abuses, including the alleged sexual abuse of civilians, provoked resentment and distrust among Haitians.[^cite_note-199][^cite_note-200][^cite_note-Encylopedia_Britannica_-_Haiti-56] [Boniface Alexandre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boniface_Alexandre) assumed interim authority until 2006, when René Préval was re-elected president following the [2006 elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_Haitian_general_election) with 55% voter turnout.[^cite_note-\:2-201][^cite_note-Bradt20-198][^cite_note-Encylopedia_Britannica_-_Haiti-56][^cite_note-thompson2006-202]

#### Post-Aristide era (2004–present)

See also: [Gang war in Haiti](72691539.md) and [Haitian crisis (2018–present)](59942434.md)

Amidst the continuing political chaos, a series of natural disasters hit Haiti. In 2004, [Tropical Storm Jeanne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurricane_Jeanne#Haiti) skimmed the north coast, leaving 3,006 people dead in flooding and mudslides, mostly in Gonaïves.[^cite_note-203] In 2008, Haiti was again struck by tropical storms; [Tropical Storm Fay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropical_Storm_Fay_\(2008\)), [Hurricane Gustav](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurricane_Gustav), [Hurricane Hanna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurricane_Hanna_\(2008\)) and [Hurricane Ike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurricane_Ike) all produced heavy winds and rain, resulting in 331 deaths and about 800,000 in need of humanitarian aid.[^cite_note-204] The state of affairs produced by these storms was intensified by already high food and fuel prices that had caused a food crisis and political unrest in April 2008.[^cite_note-205][^cite_note-206][^cite_note-Encylopedia_Britannica_-_Haiti-56]



The [Haitian National Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Palace_\(Haiti\)), located in Port-au-Prince, was heavily damaged after the [earthquake of 2010](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_Haiti_earthquake), and officially demolished in 2012. It was originally a two-story structure; the second story completely collapsed.

On 12 January 2010, Haiti was struck by a magnitude-[7.0 earthquake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_Haiti_earthquake). This was the country's most severe earthquake in over 200 years.[^cite_note-207] The earthquake was reported to have left between 160,000 and 316,000 people dead and up to 1.6 million homeless, making it one of the [deadliest natural disasters ever recorded](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_natural_disasters_by_death_toll).[^cite_note-208][^cite_note-209][^cite_note-210] An estimated 80% of schools and more than half of Haiti's hospitals were destroyed or damaged.[^cite_note-\:2-201] The situation was exacerbated by a subsequent massive [cholera outbreak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010s_Haiti_cholera_outbreak) that was triggered when cholera-infected waste from a United Nations peacekeeping station contaminated the country's main river, the [Artibonite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artibonite_River).[^cite_note-Bradt20-198][^cite_note-211][^cite_note-212] In 2017, it was reported that roughly 10,000 Haitians had died and nearly a million had been made ill. After years of denial, the United Nations apologized in 2016, but as of 2017, they have refused to acknowledge fault, thus avoiding financial responsibility.[^cite_note-213]

General elections had been planned for January 2010, but were postponed due to the earthquake.[^cite_note-Encylopedia_Britannica_-_Haiti-56] [Elections were held on 28 November 2010](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010%E2%80%9311_Haitian_general_election) for the senate, the parliament and the first round of the presidential elections. The run-off between [Michel Martelly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michel_Martelly) and [Mirlande Manigat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mirlande_Manigat) declared Martelly the winner.[^cite_note-Bradt21-214][^cite_note-215] In 2011, both former presidents Jean-Claude Duvalier and Jean-Bertrand Aristide returned to Haiti; attempts to try Duvalier for crimes committed under his rule were shelved following his death in 2014.[^cite_note-216][^cite_note-217][^cite_note-Kushner2011-218][^cite_note-Bradt21-214] In 2013, the Haitian government called for European governments to pay [reparations for slavery](38009444.md) and establish an official commission for the settlement of past wrongdoings.[^cite_note-219][^cite_note-220] Meanwhile, after continuing political wrangling with the opposition and allegations of electoral fraud, Martelly agreed to step down in 2016 without a successor in place.[^cite_note-Bradt21-214][^cite_note-221] After numerous postponements, partly owing to the effects of devastating [Hurricane Matthew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurricane_Matthew), elections were held [in November 2016](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/November_2016_Haitian_presidential_election) with a record low voter turnout of just 17%.[^cite_note-\:2-201][^cite_note-222][^cite_note-223] The victor, [Jovenel Moïse](48475064.md) of the [Haitian Tèt Kale Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haitian_T%C3%A8t_Kale_Party), was sworn in as president in 2017.[^cite_note-224][^cite_note-225]

Protests began on 7 July 2018 in response to increased fuel prices. Over time, these protests evolved into demands for the resignation of president Moïse.[^cite_note-226] On 7 July 2021, [Moïse was assassinated](68178591.md) in an attack on his private residence, and First Lady [Martine Moïse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martine_Mo%C3%AFse) was hospitalized in [Miami](53846.md).[^cite_note-227] Amid the political crisis, the government of Haiti installed [Ariel Henry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ariel_Henry) as the acting prime minister on 20 July.[^cite_note-haitifactbook-228][^cite_note-229] On 14 August, Haiti suffered [another huge earthquake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Haiti_earthquake), with many casualties.[^cite_note-230] The earthquake also damaged Haiti's economic conditions and led to a rise in gang violence which by September 2021 had escalated to a long-lasting full-blown gang war and other violent crimes within the country.[^cite_note-231][^cite_note-232] As of March 2022, Haiti still has no president, no parliamentary quorum, and a dysfunctional high court due to a lack of judges.[^cite_note-haitifactbook-228] [2022 protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Haitian_fuel_protests) against the government and [rising fuel prices](68934947.md) intensified.[^cite_note-233][^cite_note-234]

By late 2023, gangs and armed groups controlled an estimated 80% of the capital, Port-au-Prince.[^cite_note-\:2-201] During 2023, gangs killed 4,789 people, kidnapped 2,490 more and displaced an estimated 362,000 from their homes (mostly in Port-au-Prince).[^cite_note-\:2-201][^cite_note-235] Doctors, lawyers, and other wealthy members of society were kidnapped and held for ransom.[^cite_note-kidnapping-236] Many victims were killed when ransom demands were not met, leading those with the means to do so to flee the country, further hampering efforts to pull the country out of the crisis.[^cite_note-kidnapping-236] It is estimated that amidst the crisis up to 20% of qualified medical staff had left Haiti by the end of 2023.[^cite_note-medical_crisis-237]

In March 2024, Ariel Henry was prevented by gangs from returning to Haiti, following a visit to [Kenya](188171.md).[^cite_note-238] Henry agreed to resign once a transitional government had been formed. As of that month, nearly half of Haiti's population was living under [acute food insecurity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_famine_in_Haiti), according to the [World Food Programme](33582.md).[^cite_note-\:1-26] On 25 April 2024, the [Transitional Presidential Council](76614454.md) took over the governance of Haiti and remained in power until 7 February 2026, after no presidential election was held.[^cite_note-sworn_in-239] [Michel Patrick Boisvert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michel_Patrick_Boisvert) was named acting prime minister.[^cite_note-sworn_in-239] On 3 June 2024, the council swore in [Garry Conille](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garry_Conille) as acting prime minister.[^cite_note-240] On 10 November, [Alix Didier Fils-Aimé](78331060.md) replaced Conille as acting prime minister.[^cite_note-241] Under Fils-Aimé, [elections are scheduled](59684636.md) to be held in late November to early December of 2026.[^cite_note-242]

## Geography

Main article: [Geography of Haiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Haiti)



Topographical map of Haiti

Haiti forms the western three-eighths of Hispaniola, the second largest island in the [Greater Antilles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Antilles). At 27,750 km<sup>2</sup> (10,710 sq mi) Haiti is the third largest country in the Caribbean behind Cuba and the Dominican Republic, the latter sharing a 360-kilometer (224 mi) [border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominican_Republic%E2%80%93Haiti_border) with Haiti. The country has a roughly horseshoe shape and because of this it has a disproportionately long coastline, second in length (1,771 km or 1,100 mi) behind Cuba in the Greater Antilles.[^cite_note-243][^cite_note-244]

Haiti is the most mountainous country in the Caribbean. Its terrain consists of mountains interspersed with small coastal plains and river valleys.[^cite_note-CIA_World_Factbook_–_Haiti-245] Plains are only 22% of its territory.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMetz2001315-246] The climate is tropical, with some variation depending on altitude. The highest point is [Pic la Selle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pic_la_Selle), at 2,680 meters (8,793 ft).[^cite_note-NgCheong-Lum,_Roseline_19-247][^cite_note-CIA_World_Factbook_–_Haiti-245][^cite_note-Encylopedia_Britannica_-_Haiti-56]

The northern region or **Marien Region** consists of the *[Massif du Nord](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massif_du_Nord)* (Northern Massif) and the *[Plaine du Nord](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaine_du_Nord)* (Northern Plain). The *Massif du Nord* is an extension of the *Cordillera Central* in the Dominican Republic.[^cite_note-Encylopedia_Britannica_-_Haiti-56] It begins at Haiti's eastern border, north of the [Guayamouc River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guayamouc_River), and extends to the northwest through the northern peninsula. The lowlands of the *Plaine du Nord* lie along the northern border with the Dominican Republic, between the *Massif du Nord* and the North Atlantic Ocean.

The central region or **Artibonite Region** consists of two plains and two sets of mountain ranges. The *Plateau Central* (Central Plateau) extends along both sides of the Guayamouc River, south of the *Massif du Nord*. It runs from the southeast to the northwest. To the southwest of the *Plateau Central* are the *[Montagnes Noires](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montagnes_Noires,_Haiti)*, whose most northwestern part merges with the *Massif du Nord*. The most important valley in terms of crops is the Plaine de l'Artibonite, which lies between the Montagnes Noires and the Chaîne des Matheux.[^cite_note-Encylopedia_Britannica_-_Haiti-56] This region contains the country's longest river, the [Riviere l'Artibonite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artibonite_River), which begins in the western region of the Dominican Republic and continues for most of its length through central Haiti, where it then empties into the [Golfe de la Gonâve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_of_Gon%C3%A2ve).[^cite_note-Encylopedia_Britannica_-_Haiti-56] Also in this valley lies Haiti's second largest lake, [Lac de Péligre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lac_de_P%C3%A9ligre), formed as a result of the construction of the [Péligre Dam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P%C3%A9ligre_Dam) in the mid-1950s.[^cite_note-Wells-248]



Saint-Marc Arrondissement, [Artibonite Department](489220.md)

The southern region or **Xaragua Region** consists of the *[Plaine du Cul-de-Sac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaine_du_Cul-de-Sac)* (the southeast) and the mountainous southern peninsula (the [Tiburon Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tiburon_Peninsula,_Haiti)). The Plaine du Cul-de-Sac is a natural depression that harbors the country's saline lakes, such as [Trou Caïman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trou_Ca%C3%AFman) and Haiti's largest lake, [Étang Saumatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89tang_Saumatre). The [Chaîne de la Selle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cha%C3%AEne_de_la_Selle) mountain range – an extension of the southern mountain chain of the Dominican Republic (the Sierra de Baoruco) – extends from the Massif de la Selle in the east to the [Massif de la Hotte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massif_de_la_Hotte) in the west.[^cite_note-Encylopedia_Britannica_-_Haiti-56]

Haiti includes several offshore islands. The island of [Tortuga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tortuga_\(Haiti\)) is located off the coast of northern Haiti. The [arrondissement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arrondissements_of_Haiti) of [La Gonâve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gon%C3%A2ve_Island) is located on the island of the same name, in the Golfe de la Gonâve; Haiti's largest island, Gonâve is moderately populated by rural villagers. [Île à Vache](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%8Ele_%C3%A0_Vache) is located off the southwest coast; also part of Haiti are the [Cayemites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cayemites), located in the Gulf of Gonâve north of [Pestel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pestel,_Grand'Anse). [Navassa Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Navassa_Island), located 40 nautical miles (46 mi; 74 km) west of [Jérémie](1714154.md) on the southwest peninsula,[^cite_note-249] is subject to an ongoing territorial dispute with the United States, who currently administer the island.[^cite_note-USGS-250]

### Climate



Köppen climate types of Haiti

The climate is tropical with some variation depending on altitude.[^cite_note-CIA_World_Factbook_–_Haiti-245] Port-au-Prince ranges in January from an average minimum of 23 °C (73.4 °F) to an average maximum of 31 °C (87.8 °F); in July, from 25–35 °C (77–95 °F). The rainfall pattern is varied, with rain heavier in some of the lowlands and the northern and eastern slopes of the mountains. The dry season occurs from November to January.

Port-au-Prince receives an average annual rainfall of 1,370 mm (53.9 in). There are two rainy seasons, April–June and October–November. Haiti is subject to periodic droughts and floods, made more severe by deforestation. Hurricanes are a menace, and the country is prone to flooding and earthquakes.[^cite_note-CIA_World_Factbook_–_Haiti-245]

### Geology



[Saut-d'Eau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saut-d'Eau) waterfall

There are [blind thrust faults](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thrust_fault#Blind_thrust_faults) associated with the [Enriquillo-Plantain Garden fault system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enriquillo-Plantain_Garden_fault_zone) over which Haiti lies.[^cite_note-USGSHaiti2-251] After the earthquake of 2010, there was no evidence of [surface rupture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surface_rupture), and geologists' findings were based on seismological, geological and ground deformation data.[^cite_note-Hayes-252] The northern boundary of the fault is where the [Caribbean plate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caribbean_plate) shifts eastwards by about 20 mm (0.79 inches) per year in relation to the [North American plate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_American_plate). The [strike-slip fault](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strike-slip_fault) system in the region has two branches in Haiti, the [Septentrional-Oriente fault](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Septentrional-Oriente_fault_zone) in the north and the Enriquillo-Plantain Garden fault in the south.

A 2007 earthquake hazard study notes that the Enriquillo-Plantain Garden fault zone could be at the end of its seismic cycle and concludes that a worst-case forecast would involve a 7.2 M<sub>w</sub> earthquake, similar in size to the [1692 Jamaica earthquake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1692_Jamaica_earthquake).[^cite_note-DeMets-253] A study team performing a hazard assessment of the fault system recommended "high priority" historical geologic rupture studies, as the fault was fully locked and had recorded few earthquakes in the preceding 40 years.[^cite_note-18cgc-254] A magnitude 7.0 earthquake happened on this fault zone on 12 January 2010.[^cite_note-255]

Haiti has rare elements such as gold, which can be found at the [Mont Organisé](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mont_Organis%C3%A9) [gold mine](1291393.md).[^cite_note-256] There are no currently active volcanoes; no volcanic activity has been experienced since the [Miocene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miocene) age.[^cite_note-257]

### Environment

Main articles: [Environment of Haiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environment_of_Haiti) and [Deforestation in Haiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deforestation_in_Haiti)



Haiti's border with the [Dominican Republic](8060.md) in 2002, showing the extent of deforestation on the Haitian side (left)

The [soil erosion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erosion) released from the upper [catchments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drainage_basin) and deforestation have caused periodic and severe flooding, as experienced, for example, on 17 September 2004. Earlier in May that year, floods had killed over 3,000 people on Haiti's southern border with the Dominican Republic.[^cite_note-258]

Haiti's forests covered 60% of the country as recently as 50 years ago, but that has been halved to a current estimate of 30% tree cover.
