This estimate poses a stark difference from the erroneous figure of 2% which has been oft-cited in discourse concerning the country's environmental condition.[^cite_note-259] Haiti had a 2019 [Forest Landscape Integrity Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forest_Landscape_Integrity_Index) mean score of 4.01/10, ranking it 137th globally out of 172 countries.[^cite_note-FLII-Supplementary-260] Major [national parks in Haiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_national_parks_of_Haiti) include the [La Visite National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Visite_National_Park) and the [Pic Macaya National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pic_Macaya_National_Park).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMetz2001323-261]

Scientists at the [Columbia University's Center for International Earth Science Information Network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Earth_Institute#Center_for_International_Earth_Science_Information_Network_\(CIESIN\)Earth_Institute) and the [United Nations Environment Programme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Environment_Programme) are working on the Haiti Regenerative Initiative, an initiative aiming to reduce poverty and natural disaster vulnerability through ecosystem restoration and sustainable resource management.[^cite_note-262]

#### Biodiversity

Main article: [Wildlife of Haiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wildlife_of_Haiti)



The endangered Hispaniolan solenodon, endemic to the island

Haiti is home to four ecoregions: [Hispaniolan moist forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispaniolan_moist_forests), [Hispaniolan dry forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispaniolan_dry_forests), [Hispaniolan pine forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispaniolan_pine_forests), and [Greater Antilles mangroves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Antilles_mangroves).[^cite_note-DinersteinOlson2017-263] Despite its small size, the mountainous terrain and resultant multiple climatic zones have resulted in a wide variety of plant life.[^cite_note-Bradt6-264] About 5,000 species of plants have been identified in Haiti, including over 3,000 woody plants, trees, and shrubs, 600 species of [fern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fern), and 160 [orchids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orchid).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMetz2001322-265] Notable tree species include the [breadfruit tree](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breadfruit_tree), [mango tree](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mango_tree), [acacia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acacia), [mahogany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahogany), [coconut palm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coconut_palm), [royal palm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_palm) and [West Indian cedar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cedrela_odorata).[^cite_note-Bradt6-264]

Most mammal species are not native, having been brought to the island since colonial times.[^cite_note-Bradt6-264] However, there are various native [bat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bat) species, as well as the endemic [Hispaniolan hutia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispaniolan_hutia) and [Hispaniolan solenodon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispaniolan_solenodon).[^cite_note-Bradt6-264] Whale and dolphin species can be found off the coast. There are over 260 species of birds, 31 endemic to Hispaniola.[^cite_note-Bradt4-266] Notable endemic species include the [Hispaniolan trogon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispaniolan_trogon), [Hispaniolan parakeet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispaniolan_parakeet), [grey-crowned tanager](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grey-crowned_tanager) and the [Hispaniolan amazon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispaniolan_amazon).[^cite_note-Bradt4-266] There are also several [raptors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raptor_\(bird\)), as well as pelicans, ibis, hummingbirds and ducks. Reptiles are common, with species such as the [rhinoceros iguana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhinoceros_iguana), [Haitian boa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chilabothrus_fordii), [American crocodile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_crocodile) and [gecko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gecko).[^cite_note-Bradt5-267]

## Government and politics

Main article: [Politics of Haiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_of_Haiti)

See also: [Foreign relations of Haiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Haiti)



*Vacant*\
[President](516058.md)



[Alix Didier Fils-Aimé](78331060.md)\
[Acting Prime Minister](3972111.md)

The government is a [semi-presidential](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semi-presidential_system) [republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic),[^cite_note-CIA_World_Factbook_–_Haiti-245] a multiparty system wherein the [president of Haiti](516058.md) is head of state and elected directly by popular [elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elections_in_Haiti) held every five years.[^cite_note-Encylopedia_Britannica_-_Haiti-56][^cite_note-268][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEConstitution_of_HaitiArticle_134-269] A president cannot serve two consecutive terms or more than two terms in total, but can be reelected for a second term after an interval.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEConstitution_of_HaitiArticle_134–3-270] The [prime minister of Haiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_minister_of_Haiti) acts as head of government and is appointed by the president, chosen from the majority party in the National Assembly.[^cite_note-Encylopedia_Britannica_-_Haiti-56][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEConstitution_of_HaitiArticle_137-271] Executive power is exercised by the president and prime minister who together constitute the government.[^cite_note-272][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEConstitution_of_HaitiArticle_133-273] The president is the nominal [commander-in-chief](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commander-in-chief) of the [Armed Forces of Haiti](24627092.md) and the [National Police](8017603.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEConstitution_of_HaitiArticles_141,_143-274]

Legislative power is vested in the two chambers of the [National Assembly of Haiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Assembly_of_Haiti), the [Senate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senate_\(Haiti\)) and the [Chamber of Deputies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chamber_of_Deputies_\(Haiti\)).[^cite_note-CIA_World_Factbook_–_Haiti-245][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEConstitution_of_HaitiArticle_88-275] Deputies and senators are both directly elected;[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEConstitution_of_HaitiArticles_89,_94-276] deputies for a term of four years[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEConstitution_of_HaitiArticle_92-277] and senators for six years.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEConstitution_of_HaitiArticle_95-278] There are three senators from each [department of Haiti](10937444.md)[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEConstitution_of_HaitiArticle_94–1-279] and at least one deputy from each [commune](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communes_of_Haiti), with larger communes having up to three deputies.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEConstitution_of_Haiti90-280] Elections are meant to be held for the entire chamber every four years,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEConstitution_of_HaitiArticle_92–3-281] and for one-third of the Senate every two years.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEConstitution_of_HaitiArticle_95–3-282] The judicial branch includes the [Supreme Court of Haiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_Haiti) (also called the Court of Cassation) and the Courts of Appeals,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEConstitution_of_HaitiArticle_173-283] with justices for the former appointed by the president from a list recommended by the Senate, and for the latter recommended by department assemblies.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEConstitution_of_HaitiArticle_175-284]

The current structure of Haiti's political system was set forth in the [Constitution of Haiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Haiti) in 1987 and was amended in 2012.[^cite_note-CIA_World_Factbook_–_Haiti-245] The constitution creates a division of executive, legislative, and judicial powers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEConstitution_of_HaitiArticle_59-285] Haitian politics have been contentious; since independence, Haiti has suffered [32 coups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_revolutions_and_coups_d'%C3%A9tat_in_Haiti).[^cite_note-286] A long history of oppression by dictators such as François Duvalier and his son Jean-Claude Duvalier has markedly affected the republic's governance and society, and since then Haiti has been transitioning to a democratic system.[^cite_note-Encylopedia_Britannica_-_Haiti-56] Jovenel Moïse, who was elected the 48th president in 2016, was assassinated in 2021 amidst an ongoing gang conflict, worsening a political crisis.[^cite_note-BTI-2024-287][^cite_note-CrisisGroup-2025-288] With the presidency vacant, and the National Assembly and judiciary no longer functioning, there have been no elected officials in Haiti since 2023, and the political architecture set by the constitution exists only on paper.[^cite_note-CIA_World_Factbook_–_Haiti-245][^cite_note-289]

The Transitional Presidential Council with nine members was created when acting prime minister Ariel Henry was forced to resign on 24 April 2024.[^cite_note-CIA_World_Factbook_–_Haiti-245][^cite_note-290][^cite_note-CrisisGroup-2025-288] The council intended to hold a two-round election in November 2025 and January 2026, to inaugurate a new government in February 2026,[^cite_note-291] but it has been undermined by corruption scandals and infighting.[^cite_note-CrisisGroup-2025-288][^cite_note-292] The Haitian government and the Kenyan-led [Multinational Security Support Mission](74972533.md), have not been able to restore public safety in the country.[^cite_note-CrisisGroup-2025-288] The [planned election](59684636.md) was further delayed until the second half of 2026 due to violence,[^cite_note-293][^cite_note-294][^cite_note-295] and the expiration of the Council's mandate on 7 February 2026 left acting prime minister Alix Didier Fils-Aimé as the sole executive until the election, with the support of several major political parties.[^cite_note-296]

Haiti is a member of a wide range of international and regional organizations, such as the United Nations, CARICOM, [Community of Latin American and Caribbean States](26313243.md), [International Monetary Fund](15251.md), [Organisation of American States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organisation_of_American_States), [Organisation internationale de la Francophonie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organisation_internationale_de_la_Francophonie), [OPANAL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OPANAL) and the [World Trade Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Trade_Organization).[^cite_note-CIA_World_Factbook_–_Haiti-245]

### Administrative divisions

Main article: [Administrative divisions of Haiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Administrative_divisions_of_Haiti)



Departments of Haiti

Haiti is divided into ten [departments](10937444.md),[^cite_note-CIA_World_Factbook_–_Haiti-245] which are the largest territorial units.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEConstitution_of_HaitiArticle_76-297] The departments are further divided into 42 [arrondissements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arrondissements_of_Haiti), 145 [communes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communes_of_Haiti) and 571 [communal sections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communal_section).[^cite_note-298][^cite_note-299][^cite_note-300] Departments and communes have autonomy from the central government in their administration and finances.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEConstitution_of_HaitiArticle_66-301][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEConstitution_of_HaitiArticle_77-302] Communal sections, communes, and departments are led by a three-member council which is elected either by universal suffrage (in communal sections) or by an assembly in communes and departments.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEConstitution_of_HaitiArticles_63,_66,_78-303] An assembly consists of representatives from each of the previous level of territorial units.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEConstitution_of_HaitiArticles_67,_80-304]

### Military and law enforcement

Main articles: [Armed Forces of Haiti](24627092.md) and [Haitian National Police](8017603.md)



Newly graduated Haitian National Police officers in 2025

The [Armed Forces of Haiti](24627092.md) (FAd'H) and the [Haitian National Police](8017603.md) (PNH) together officially make up the Public Force of Haiti,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEConstitution_of_HaitiArticle_263-305] whose personnel take an oath to the constitution and the flag of the country.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEConstitution_of_HaitiArticle_263–2-306] The Armed Forces consist of the [land](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haitian_Army), sea, and [air](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haitian_Aviation_Corps) forces, along with technical support.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEConstitution_of_HaitiArticle_264-307] They are led by an active-duty [general officer](148537.md) who is appointed as the [commander-in-chief of the Armed Forces of Haiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_commanders-in-chief_of_the_Armed_Forces_of_Haiti).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEConstitution_of_HaitiArticles_264–1,_264–2,_264–3-308] The [Ministry of Defense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Defense_\(Haiti\)) is the main oversight body of the armed forces[^cite_note-309] while the police are part of the [Ministry of Justice and Public Security](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Justice_and_Public_Security_\(Haiti\)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEConstitution_of_HaitiArticle_269-310] The constitutional duties of the FA'dH include defending the country's borders, responding to natural disasters, and providing assistance to the police.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEConstitution_of_HaitiArticle_266-311] The 1987 constitution provides the basis for conscription,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEConstitution_of_HaitiArticle_268-312] though it has never been used.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMetz2001470-313] The Armed Forces were disbanded in 1995 and security became solely the responsibility of the civilian National Police[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMetz2001468–469-314][^cite_note-Haiti:_Police_and_Law_Enforcement-315] until 2017. As of 2023 the Armed Forces had a strength of 2,000 service members, who were trained in nearby countries. The military has had a small role during the gang war, which includes protecting government officials.[^cite_note-PBS-28082024-316]

The National Police was established in 1995 to replace the military's former role in law enforcement, initially recruited from among former military personnel and Haitian migrants to the United States.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMetz2001477-317] In 1999 it had 6,000 members;[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMetz2001477-317] in 2010, it numbered 7,000;[^cite_note-318] and in 2016 it had 9,000.[^cite_note-CrisisGroup-2025-288] It is led by a director-general appointed by the president.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMetz2001478-319] The PNH consists of the Administrative Police for day-to-day police work, the Judicial Police for investigations, and some specialized units that include presidential security, riot control, a SWAT team, and the [Haitian Coast Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haitian_Coast_Guard).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMetz2001478–479-320] Under-equipped and understaffed, it has been unable maintain security in the entire Haitian territory, despite some improvements to its capability by 2012.[^cite_note-321] Since the start of the gang war in 2020 the PNH has seen increasingly high levels of desertion and casualties.[^cite_note-BTI-2024-287][^cite_note-PBS-28082024-316]

### Law and crime

Main article: [Crime in Haiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crime_in_Haiti)

The legal system is based on a modified version of the [Napoleonic Code](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleonic_Code).[^cite_note-322][^cite_note-Encylopedia_Britannica_-_Haiti-56] Haiti has consistently ranked among the most corrupt countries in the world on the [Corruption Perceptions Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corruption_Perceptions_Index).[^cite_note-323] According to a 2006 report by the [Corruption Perceptions Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corruption_Perceptions_Index), there is a strong correlation between corruption and poverty in Haiti. The republic ranked first of all countries surveyed for levels of perceived domestic corruption.[^cite_note-324] It is estimated that President "Baby Doc" Duvalier, his wife [Michele](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mich%C3%A8le_Bennett), and their agents stole US\$504 million from the treasury between 1971 and 1986.[^cite_note-325] Some media outlets alleged that millions were stolen by President Aristide.[^cite_note-American_Spectator_Vol_1994-326][^cite_note-327][^cite_note-328][^cite_note-329] The BBC described [pyramid schemes](48732.md) in which Haitians lost hundreds of millions in 2002, as the "only real economic initiative" of the Aristide years.[^cite_note-basketcase-330]

After the fall of the military government in the early 1990s, the presence of a large quantity of firearms and paramilitary groups (consisting of former soldiers, gangsters, and other criminal elements) gave Haiti one of the highest crime rates in the world as of 2005.[^cite_note-331] According to the 2023 [United Nations Office on Drugs and Crime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Office_on_Drugs_and_Crime) report, due to escalating gang violence and the near-collapse of state institutions, the security situation has deteriorated sharply. The official homicide rate for Haiti more than doubled in 2023 to approximately 40.9 per 100,000 (with 4,789 homicides reported by the UN).[^cite_note-332] According to the UN, Haiti faces a worsening crisis marked by extreme violence, political instability, and a deepening humanitarian emergency. UN Special Representative [Virginia Gamba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virginia_Gamba_\(UN_official\)) condemned the use of children by armed gangs, highlighting sexual violence as a weapon of war. The removal of Prime Minister Garry Conille has further destabilized governance. Armed groups have targeted schools and hospitals, displacing thousands and leaving 300,000 children without education. The UN calls for urgent humanitarian aid, enhanced security efforts, and political unity to address the crisis, as over 2.7 million people live under gang control.[^cite_note-333][^cite_note-334]

Port-au-Prince penitentiary is home to half of Haiti's prisoners. The prison has a capacity of 1,200 detainees, but as of November 2017 the penitentiary was obliged to keep 4,359 detainees, a 363% occupancy level.[^cite_note-auto-335] The inability to receive sufficient funds has caused deadly cases of malnutrition, combined with the tight living conditions, increases the risk of infectious diseases such as tuberculosis.[^cite_note-auto-335] Haitian law states that once arrested, one must go before a judge within 48 hours; however, this is very rare.[^cite_note-336] Unless families are able to provide the necessary funds for inmates to appear before a judge, there is a very slim chance the inmate would have a trial, on average, within 10 years.[^cite_note-337] In confined living spaces for 22–23 hours a day, inmates are not provided with latrines and are forced to defecate into plastic bags. These conditions were considered inhumane by the Inter-American Court of Human Rights in 2008.[^cite_note-prison-338] On 3 March 2024, armed gangs stormed the main prison in Port-au-Prince, and about 3,700 inmates escaped, while 12 people were killed.[^cite_note-339]

## Economy



Historical GDP per capita development

Main article: [Economy of Haiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Haiti)

The [economy of Haiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Haiti) declined dramatically after the 2010 earthquake and subsequent outbreak of cholera, with the country's nominal [GDP](12594.md) falling by 8% (from US\$12.15 billion to US\$11.18 billion).[^cite_note-CIA_20110303-2] Since then it has grown to US\$33.55 billion in nominal GDP as of 2025,[^cite_note-340] and during the same time period it grew from \$26.88 billion to \$38.2 billion in [PPP-adjusted GDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(PPP\)).[^cite_note-341] Despite its tourism industry, Haiti is one of the poorest countries in the Americas, with corruption, political instability, poor infrastructure, lack of health care and lack of education cited as the main causes.[^cite_note-CIA_World_Factbook_–_Haiti-245] Haiti ranked 145th of 182 countries in the 2010 United Nations [Human Development Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Development_Index), with 57.3% of the population being deprived in at least three of the HDI's poverty measures.[^cite_note-UNDP_2010-342] As of 2021, about 30% of the population lived in extreme poverty (making less than US\$2.15 a day), and 87.6% made less than US\$6.85. There is a division between the formal economy, which is controlled by an upper class and is concentrated in certain areas, and an informal economy that represents 90% of employment.[^cite_note-BTI-2024-287]

Haiti became a member of the [World Trade Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Trade_Organization) in 1996. It liberalized its economy in the 1980s, removing tariff and non-tariff barriers, which had an impact on domestic businesses, especially in the agricultural sector. The existence of monopolies and oligopolies in many economic markets distorts the price setting mechanism, and is why the cost of goods and services in Haiti is the highest in the region.[^cite_note-BTI-2024-287] Following the disputed 2000 election and accusations about President Aristide's rule,[^cite_note-343] U.S. aid to the Haitian government was cut off between 2001 and 2004.[^cite_note-344] After Aristide's departure in 2004, aid was restored and the [Brazilian army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_army) led a [United Nations Stabilization Mission in Haiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Stabilization_Mission_in_Haiti) peacekeeping operation. After almost four years of recession, the economy grew by 1.5% in 2005.[^cite_note-345] In September 2009, Haiti met the conditions set out by the [IMF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IMF) and [World Bank](36752.md)'s [Heavily Indebted Poor Countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heavily_Indebted_Poor_Countries) program to qualify for cancellation of its external debt.[^cite_note-346]

In 2015, more than 90 percent of the government's budget came from an agreement with [Petrocaribe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petrocaribe), a Venezuela-led oil alliance.[^cite_note-347]

### Foreign aid

Further information: [Foreign aid to Haiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_aid_to_Haiti)

Haiti received more than US\$4 billion in aid from 1990 to 2003, including US\$1.5 billion from the United States.[^cite_note-348] The largest donor is the US, followed by Canada and the European Union.[^cite_note-349] In January 2010, following the earthquake, US President [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama) promised US\$1.15 billion in assistance.[^cite_note-350] The European Union pledged more than €400 million (US\$616 million).[^cite_note-351] Dominican Republic provided extensive humanitarian aid, including the funding and construction of a public university,[^cite_note-352] human capital, free healthcare services in the border region, and logistical support after the 2010 earthquake.[^cite_note-353]

The United Nations states that US\$13.34 billion has been earmarked for post-earthquake reconstruction through 2020, though two years after the 2010 quake, less than half of that amount had actually been released. As of 2015, the US government has allocated US\$4 billion, US\$3 billion has already been spent, and the rest is dedicated to longer-term projects.[^cite_note-354]

### Trade

According to the 2015 CIA [World Factbook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Factbook), Haiti's main import partners are: Dominican Republic 35%, US 26.8%, Netherlands Antilles 8.7%, China 7% (est. 2013). Haiti's main export partner is the US 83.5% (est. 2013).[^cite_note-355] Haiti had a trade deficit of US\$3 billion in 2011, or 41% of GDP.[^cite_note-ammart-356]

### Energy

Main article: [Electricity sector in Haiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electricity_sector_in_Haiti)



Haiti electricity production by source



Power plant in Port-au-Prince

Haiti relies heavily on an oil alliance with [Petrocaribe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petrocaribe) for much of its energy requirements. In recent years, hydroelectric, solar and wind energy have been explored as possible sustainable energy sources.[^cite_note-357] As of 2017, among all the countries in the Americas, Haiti produces the least energy. Less than a quarter of the country has electric coverage.[^cite_note-358] Most regions of Haiti that do have energy are powered by generators. These generators are often expensive and produce a lot of pollution. The areas that do get electricity experience power cuts on a daily basis, and some areas are limited to 12 hours of electricity a day. Electricity is provided by a small number of independent companies: Sogener, E-power, and Haytrac.[^cite_note-359] There is no [national electricity grid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_electricity_grid).[^cite_note-\:02-360] The most common sources of energy are wood and charcoal. About 4 million metric tons of wood products are consumed yearly.[^cite_note-361] Like charcoal and wood, petroleum is also an important source of energy. Since Haiti cannot produce its own fuel, all fuel is imported. Yearly, about 691,000 tons of oil is imported into the country.[^cite_note-\:02-360]

In 2018, a 24-hour electricity project was announced; for this purpose 236 MW needs to be installed in Port-au-Prince alone, with an additional 75 MW needed in all other regions. Presently only 27.5% of the population has access to electricity; moreover, the national energy agency l'Électricité d'Haïti is only able to meet 62% of overall electricity demand.[^cite_note-362]

### Personal income



A market in [Cap-Haïtien](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cap-Ha%C3%AFtien)

Haiti suffers from a shortage of skilled labor, widespread unemployment, and underemployment. Most Haitians in the labor force have informal jobs. Three-quarters of the population lives on US\$2 or less per day.[^cite_note-\:0-363] Remittances from Haitians living abroad are the primary source of foreign exchange, equaling one-fifth (20%) of GDP and more than five times the earnings from exports as of 2012.[^cite_note-364] In 2004, 80% or more of college graduates from Haiti were living abroad.[^cite_note-365]

Occasionally, families who are unable to care for children may send them to live with a wealthier family as a *[restavek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Restavek)*, or house servant. In return the family are supposed to ensure that the child is educated and provided with food and shelter; however, the system is open to abuse and has proved controversial, with some likening it to child slavery.[^cite_note-kennedy14-366][^cite_note-367]

### Real estate

In rural areas, people often live in wooden huts with corrugated iron roofs. Outhouses are located in back of the huts. In Port-au-Prince, [shantytowns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shantytowns) surround the central city and go up the mountainsides.[^cite_note-c151222-368] The middle and upper classes live in suburbs, or in the central part of the bigger cities in apartments, where there is urban planning. Many of the houses they live in are like miniature fortresses, located behind walls embedded with metal spikes, barbed wire, broken glass, and sometimes all three. The houses have backup generators, because the electrical grid is unreliable. Some even have rooftop reservoirs for water.[^cite_note-c151222-368]

### Agriculture

Further information: [Agriculture in Haiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agriculture_in_Haiti)



Rows of cabbage, Haiti

Haiti is the world's leading producer of [vetiver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chrysopogon_zizanioides), a root plant used to make luxury perfumes, essential oils and fragrances, providing for half the world's supply.[^cite_note-369][^cite_note-370][^cite_note-371] Roughly 40–50% of Haitians work in the agricultural sector.[^cite_note-CIA_World_Factbook_–_Haiti-245][^cite_note-feedh-372] However, according to soil surveys by the United States Department of Agriculture in the early 1980s, only 11.3 percent of the land was highly suitable for crops. Haiti relies upon imports for half its food needs and 80% of its rice.[^cite_note-feedh-372]

Haiti exports crops such as [mangoes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mango), [cacao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cocoa_bean), [coffee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coffee), [papayas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Papaya), [mahogany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahogany) nuts, [spinach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spinach), and [watercress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Watercress).[^cite_note-ute.gouv.ht-373] Agricultural products constitute 6% of all exports.[^cite_note-ammart-356] In addition, local agricultural products include [maize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maize), [beans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beans), [cassava](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cassava), [sweet potato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sweet_potato), [peanuts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peanut), [pistachios](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pistachio), [bananas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banana), [millet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Millet), [pigeon peas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pigeon_pea), sugarcane, [rice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rice), [sorghum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sorghum), and [wood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wood).[^cite_note-ute.gouv.ht-373][^cite_note-374]

### Currency

The [Haitian gourde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haitian_gourde) (HTG) is the national currency. The "Haitian dollar" equates to 5 gourdes (*goud*). The vast majority of the business sector and individuals will also accept US dollars, though at the outdoor markets gourdes may be preferred. Locals may refer to the USD as "dollar américain" (*dola ameriken*) or "dollar US" (pronounced *oo-es*).[^cite_note-375]

### Tourism

Main article: [Tourism in Haiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism_in_Haiti)



[Labadee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labadee), a cruise ship destination

The tourism market in Haiti is undeveloped, and the government is heavily promoting this sector. Haiti has many of the features that attract tourists to other Caribbean destinations, such as white sand beaches, mountainous scenery and a year-round warm climate. However, the country's poor image overseas, at times exaggerated, has hampered the development of this sector.[^cite_note-Encylopedia_Britannica_-_Haiti-56] In 2014, the country received 1,250,000 tourists (mostly from cruise ships), and the industry generated US\$200 million in 2014.

Several hotels were opened in 2014, including an upscale [Best Western Premier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Best_Western#Differentiation),[^cite_note-376][^cite_note-377] a five-star Royal Oasis hotel by Occidental Hotel and Resorts in [Pétion-Ville](10671007.md),[^cite_note-378][^cite_note-379][^cite_note-380] a four-star [Marriott Hotel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marriott_International) in the Turgeau area of Port-au-Prince[^cite_note-381] and other new hotel developments in Port-au-Prince, [Les Cayes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Les_Cayes), [Cap-Haïtien](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cap-Ha%C3%AFtien) and [Jacmel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacmel).

### Caracol Industrial Park

On 21 October 2012, Haitian President Michel Martelly, US Secretary of State [Hillary Clinton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hillary_Clinton), Bill Clinton, [Richard Branson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Branson), [Ben Stiller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ben_Stiller) and [Sean Penn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sean_Penn) inaugurated the 240-hectare (600-acre) Caracol industrial park, the largest in the Caribbean.[^cite_note-usatoday.com-382] The project cost US\$300 million and included a 10-megawatt [power plant](212141.md), a water-treatment plant and worker housing.[^cite_note-usatoday.com-382] The plan for the park pre-dated the 2010 earthquake but was fast-tracked as part of US foreign aid strategy to help Haiti recover.[^cite_note-bostonreview-383] The park was part of a "master plan" for Haiti's North and North-East departments, including the expansion of the [Cap-Haïtien International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cap-Ha%C3%AFtien_International_Airport) to accommodate large international flights, the construction of an international seaport in [Fort-Liberté](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort-Libert%C3%A9) and the opening of the \$50 million Roi Henri Christophe Campus of a new university in Limonade (near Cap-Haïtien) on 12 January 2012.[^cite_note-384]

In 2012, [USAID](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Agency_for_International_Development) believed the park had the potential to create as many as 65,000 jobs once fully developed.[^cite_note-385][^cite_note-386] South Korean clothing manufacturer [Sae-A Trading Co. Ltd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sae-A_Trading_Co._Ltd), the park's only major tenant, created 5,000 permanent jobs out of the 20,000 it had projected and promised to build 5,000 houses yet only 750 homes had been built near Caracol by 2014.[^cite_note-bostonreview-383]

Ten years later, the park was considered to have failed to uphold its promise to deliver the transformation the Clintons had promised.[^cite_note-guardiancaracol-387] The US invested tens of millions of dollars into the port project but eventually abandoned it.[^cite_note-guardiancaracol-387] In order to establish the park, hundreds of families of small farmers had to be removed from the land, approximately 3,500 people overall.[^cite_note-buzzfeed-388] An audit by the [United States Government Accountability Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Government_Accountability_Office) uncovered that the port project lacked "staff with technical expertise in planning, construction, and oversight of a port" and revealed that USAID had not constructed a port anywhere since the 1970s.[^cite_note-guardiancaracol-387] A USAID feasibility study in 2015 found that "a new port was not viable for a variety of technical, environmental and economic reasons", that the US was short US\$72m in funds to cover the majority of the projected costs, and that private companies USAID had wanted to attract "had no interest in supporting the construction of a new port in northern Haiti".[^cite_note-guardiancaracol-387]

### Transportation

Main article: [Transport in Haiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transport_in_Haiti)



Rail map as of 1925

Haiti has two main highways that run from one end of the country to the other. The northern highway, Route Nationale No. 1 (National Highway One), originates in Port-au-Prince, winding through the coastal towns of [Montrouis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montrouis) and [Gonaïves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gona%C3%AFves), before reaching its terminus at the northern port [Cap-Haïtien](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cap-Ha%C3%AFtien). The southern highway, Route Nationale No. 2, links Port-au-Prince with [Les Cayes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Les_Cayes) via [Léogâne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L%C3%A9og%C3%A2ne) and [Petit-Goâve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petit-Go%C3%A2ve). The state of roads is generally poor, many being potholed and becoming impassable in rough weather.[^cite_note-Encylopedia_Britannica_-_Haiti-56]

[Port international de Port-au-Prince](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_international_de_Port-au-Prince) has more registered shipping than any of the other dozen ports in the country. The port's facilities include cranes, large berths, and warehouses, but these facilities are not in good condition. The port is underused, possibly due to the substantially high port fees. The port of [Saint-Marc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint-Marc) is currently the preferred port of entry for consumer goods.[^cite_note-389]

In the past, Haiti used rail transport; however, the rail infrastructure was poorly maintained when in use and cost of rehabilitation is beyond the means of the Haitian economy. In 2018 the Regional Development Council of the Dominican Republic proposed a "trans-Hispaniola" railway between both countries.[^cite_note-390]

#### Airports



[Toussaint Louverture International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toussaint_Louverture_International_Airport)

Main article: [List of airports in Haiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_airports_in_Haiti)

[Toussaint Louverture International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toussaint_Louverture_International_Airport), located ten kilometers (six miles) north-northeast of Port-au-Prince proper in the commune of [Tabarre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tabarre), is the primary hub for entry and exit into the country. It has Haiti's main jetway, and along with [Cap-Haïtien International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cap-Ha%C3%AFtien_International_Airport) handles the vast majority of the country's international flights. Cities such as Jacmel, Jérémie, Les Cayes, and Port-de-Paix have smaller, less accessible airports that are serviced by regional airlines and private aircraft.

In 2013, plans for the development of an international airport on Île-à-Vache were introduced by the prime minister.[^cite_note-391]

In May 2024, the airport reopened following three months closure following violence, and is expected to help ease a shortage of medications and basic supplies.[^cite_note-392][^cite_note-393]

#### Bus service



A "tap tap" bus in [Port-Salut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port-Salut)

[Tap tap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tap_tap) buses are colorfully painted buses or pick-up trucks that serve as shared taxis. The "tap tap" name comes from the sound of passengers tapping on the metal bus body to indicate they want off.[^cite_note-394] These vehicles for hire are often privately owned and extensively decorated. They follow fixed routes, do not leave until filled with passengers, and riders can usually disembark at any point. The decorations are a typically Haitian form of art.[^cite_note-395]

### Communications

Main articles: [Telecommunications in Haiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telecommunications_in_Haiti) and [Television in Haiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Television_in_Haiti)

Communications include the radio, television, fixed and mobile telephones, and the Internet. Haiti ranked last among North American countries in the World Economic Forum's Network Readiness Index (NRI) –  an indicator for determining the development level of a country's information and communication technologies. Haiti ranked number 143 out of 148 overall in the 2014 NRI ranking, down from 141 in 2013.[^cite_note-396]

### Water supply and sanitation

Main article: [Water supply and sanitation in Haiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_supply_and_sanitation_in_Haiti)

[^cite_note-397]Haiti faces key challenges in the water supply and sanitation sector. Access to these public services are very low, with 26% of Haitians having no access to improved water sources, and 7 out of 10 people lacking access to proper sanitation systems.[^cite_note-398] Quality is inadequate, as public institutions remain very weak despite foreign aid and the government's declared intent to strengthen the sector's institutions. Foreign and Haitian [NGOs](46539.md) play an important role in the sector, especially in rural and urban slum areas.[^cite_note-399]

## Demographics

Main article: [Demographics of Haiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Haiti)



Haiti's population (1800–2021)

In 2018, Haiti's population was estimated to be about 10,788,000.[^cite_note-CIA_World_Factbook_–_Haiti-245] In 2006, half of the population was younger than age 20.[^cite_note-400] In 1950, the first formal census gave a total population of 3.1 million.[^cite_note-401] Haiti averages approximately 350 people per square kilometer (910 people/mi<sup>2</sup>), with its population concentrated most heavily in urban areas, coastal plains, and valleys.



A street scene of Port-au-Princiens with a [UN Peacekeeper of the Haiti mission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Stabilisation_Mission_in_Haiti)

There is a large [Haitian diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haitian_diaspora) community, predominantly based in the [United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haitian_Americans), [Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haitian_Canadians), and [France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Haitians).[^cite_note-402] Many others of Haitian descent live abroad in the Dominican Republic, [Cuba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haitian_Cuban), the Bahamas, the French Antilles, the Turks and Caicos, Jamaica, Puerto Rico, Venezuela, Brazil, Suriname and French Guiana. There were an estimated 881,500 people of Haitian ancestry in the United States in 2015,[^cite_note-403] while in the Dominican Republic there were an estimated 800,000 in 2007.[^cite_note-pinadep-404] There were 300,000 in Cuba in 2013,[^cite_note-405] 100,000 in Canada in 2006,[^cite_note-406] 80,000 in Metropolitan France (2010),[^cite_note-407] and up to 80,000 in the Bahamas (2009).[^cite_note-408] Many influential early American settlers and black freemen, including [Jean Baptiste Point du Sable](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean_Baptiste_Point_du_Sable) and [W. E. B. Du Bois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/W._E._B._Du_Bois), were of Haitian origin.[^cite_note-Kinzie_1856_190-409][^cite_note-Meehan_1963_445-410][^cite_note-Cohn2009-411][^cite_note-412]

In 2018, the life expectancy at birth was 63.66 years.[^cite_note-413]

### Racial discrimination

Main article: [Gens de couleur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gens_de_couleur)

Under colonial rule, [Haitian mulattoes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mulatto) were generally privileged above the black majority, though they possessed fewer rights than the white population. Following the country's independence, they became the nation's social elite. Numerous leaders throughout Haiti's history have been mulattoes. During this time, the enslaved persons and the [affranchis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Affranchi) were given limited opportunities toward education, income, and occupations, but even after gaining independence, the social structure remains a legacy today as the disparity between the upper and lower classes have not been reformed significantly since the colonial days.[^cite_note-414] Making up 5% of the nation's population, mulattoes have retained their preeminence, evident in the political, economic, social and cultural hierarchy in Haiti.[^cite_note-415] As a result, the elite class today consists of a small group of influential people who are generally light in color.[^cite_note-416]

### Languages

The two official languages of Haiti are [French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_language) and [Haitian Creole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haitian_Creole). French is the principal written and administratively authorized language (as well as the main language of the press) and is spoken by 42% of [Haitians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haitians).[^cite_note-417][^cite_note-418] It is spoken by all educated Haitians, is the medium of instruction in most schools, and is used in the business sector. It is also used in ceremonial events such as weddings, graduations and church Masses. Haiti is one of two independent nations in the Americas (along with Canada) to designate French as an [official language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Official_language); the other French-speaking areas are all [overseas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_overseas_departments_and_territories) *[départements](38523.md)*, or *[collectivités](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overseas_collectivity)*, of France, such as [French Guiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Guiana). Haitian Creole is spoken by nearly all of the Haitian population. French, the base language for Haitian Creole, is popular among the Haitian elite and upper classes. French is also popular in the business sector, and to a far lesser degree, English due to [American influence](15984926.md). Spanish is spoken by some Haitians who live along the [Haitian-Dominican border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominican_Republic%E2%80%93Haiti_border).[^cite_note-419] English and Spanish may also be spoken by Haitian deportees from the United States and various Latin American countries. Overall, about 90–95% of Haitians only speak Haitian Creole and French fluently, with over half only knowing Creole.[^cite_note-420]

Haitian Creole,[^cite_note-421] locally called *Kreyòl*,[^cite_note-422] has recently undergone standardization and is spoken by virtually the entire population.[^cite_note-423] One of the [French-based creole languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French-based_creole_languages), Haitian Creole has a vocabulary overwhelmingly derived from French, but its grammar resembles that of some West African languages. It also has influences from [Taino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ta%C3%ADno), [Spanish](26825.md), and [Portuguese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_language).[^cite_note-424] Haitian Creole is related to the other French creoles, and in particular to the [Antillean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antillean_Creole) and [Louisiana Creole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_Creole_French) variants.

### Religion

Main article: [Religion in Haiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Haiti)

Religion in Haiti according to the [Pew Research Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center) (2010)

[^cite_note-425]

1. Catholicism (56.8%)

2. Protestantism (29.6%)

3. Unaffiliated (10.6%)

4. Other (3.00%)

The 2018 [CIA World Factbook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CIA_World_Factbook) reported that 55% of Haitians were [Catholics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholicism_in_Haiti) and 29% were [Protestants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestantism_in_Haiti) (Baptist 15.4%, Pentecostal 7.9%, [Seventh-day Adventist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seventh-day_Adventist) 3%, Methodist 1.5%, other 0.7%). Other sources put the Protestant population higher, suggesting that it might have formed one-third of the population in 2001.[^cite_note-ReyStepick2013-426] Like other countries in the neighboring Latin American region, Haiti has witnessed a general Protestant expansion, which is largely [Evangelical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evangelical_Protestant) and [Pentecostal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pentecostal) in nature.[^cite_note-427][^cite_note-428][^cite_note-429] Haitian [Cardinal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cardinal_\(Catholicism\)) [Chibly Langlois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chibly_Langlois) is president of the National Bishops Conference of the Catholic Church.[^cite_note-430]

[Vodou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haitian_vodou), a religion with West African roots similar to those of Cuba and Brazil, is formally practiced by 2.1% of the population; however, it is estimated that 50–80% of Haitians incorporate some elements of Vodou belief or practices into their religion, particularly with Catholicism. This reflects Vodou's colonial origins, when enslaved persons were obliged to disguise their traditional [loa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loa) (*lwa*), or spirits, as Catholic saints, as part of a process called [syncretism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syncretism). As such, it is difficult to estimate the number of Vodouists in Haiti,[^cite_note-431][^cite_note-432] especially given the legacy of historic persecution and misrepresentation in popular media and culture, as well as modern stigmatization among segments of the growing Protestant population. Nonetheless, Vodou was officially recognized by the Haitian government in 2003.[^cite_note-CIA_World_Factbook_–_Haiti-245] Today, Vodou is experiencing somewhat of a resurgence with younger Haitians, and some are turning to Vodou to treat mental illness.[^cite_note-433][^cite_note-434]

Reflecting the ubiquity of Vodou culture and beliefs, while many Catholics and Protestants in Haiti denounce Vodou as *[devil worship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theistic_Satanism)*, they do not deny the power or existence of its spirits; rather, they are regarded as "evil" and "[satanic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satanism)" adversaries that require intervention through [Christian prayer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_prayer). Protestants view Catholic veneration of saints as [idol worship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Idolatry), and some Protestants would often destroy statues and other Catholic paraphernalia.[^cite_note-435]

Minority religions in Haiti include [Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Haiti), [Bahá'í Faith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bah%C3%A1'%C3%AD_Faith_in_Haiti), [Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_Haiti), and [Buddhism](3267529.md).[^cite_note-CIA_20110303-2]

### Education

Main article: [Education in Haiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Haiti)



The [Universite Roi Henri Christophe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universit%C3%A9_d'%C3%89tat_d'Ha%C3%AFti,_Campus_Henri_Christophe_de_Limonade) in [Limonade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Limonade)

The educational system is based on the [French system](379033.md). Higher education, under the responsibility of the Ministry of Education,[^cite_note-436] is provided by universities and other public and private institutions.[^cite_note-437]

More than 80% of primary schools are privately managed by nongovernmental organizations, churches, communities, and for-profit operators, with minimal government oversight.[^cite_note-438] According to the 2013 Millennium Development Goals Report, Haiti has steadily boosted net enrollment rate in primary education from 47% in 1993 to 88% in 2011, achieving equal participation of boys and girls in education.[^cite_note-439] Charity organizations, including [Food for the Poor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Food_for_the_Poor) and [Haitian Health Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haitian_Health_Foundation), are building schools for children and providing necessary school supplies. According to UNESCO, Haiti's adult literacy rate was 61.7% in 2022.[^cite_note-440]

Many reformers have advocated the creation of a free, public and universal education system for all primary school-age students in Haiti. The [Inter-American Development Bank](382592.md) estimates that the government will need at least US\$3 billion to create an adequately funded system.[^cite_note-441]

Upon successful graduation of secondary school, students may continue into [higher education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Higher_education). The higher education schools include the [University of Haiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Haiti). There are also [medical schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_medical_schools_in_the_Caribbean) and [law schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legal_education) offered at both the University of Haiti and abroad. [Brown University](4157.md) is cooperating with L'Hôpital Saint-Damien to coordinate a [pediatric health care](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pediatrics) curriculum.[^cite_note-442]

### Health

Main article: [Health in Haiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_in_Haiti)

As of 2012, 60% of children under the age of 10 were [vaccinated](32653.md),[^cite_note-443][^cite_note-444] compared to 93–95% in other countries.[^cite_note-445] Recently there have been mass vaccination campaigns claiming to vaccinate as many as 91% of a target population against specific diseases (measles and rubella in this case).[^cite_note-446] Most people have no transportation or access to [Haitian hospitals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_hospitals_in_Haiti).[^cite_note-447]

The [World Health Organization](33583.md) cites [diarrheal](53951.md) diseases, [HIV/AIDS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HIV/AIDS), [meningitis](21009963.md), and respiratory infections as common causes of death.[^cite_note-448] Ninety percent of children suffer from [waterborne diseases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waterborne_disease) and [intestinal parasites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intestinal_parasite).[^cite_note-449] HIV infection is found in 1.71% of Haiti's population (est. 2015).[^cite_note-450] Per a 2017 report, incidence of [tuberculosis](30653.md) is the highest in the region with an estimated 200 cases per 100,000 people.[^cite_note-451] Approximately 30,000 Haitians fall ill with [malaria](20423.md) each year.[^cite_note-452]

Roughly 75% of households lack running water. Unsafe water, along with inadequate housing and unsanitary living conditions, contributes to the high incidence of infectious diseases. There is a chronic shortage of health care personnel and hospitals lack resources, a situation that became readily apparent after the January 2010 earthquake.[^cite_note-453] The [infant mortality rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infant_mortality_rate) in 2019 was 48.2 deaths per 1,000 live births, compared to 5.6 per 1,000 in the United States.[^cite_note-454]

After the 2010 earthquake, [Partners In Health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partners_In_Health) founded the [Hôpital Universitaire de Mirebalais](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H%C3%B4pital_Universitaire_de_Mirebalais), the largest [solar-powered](13690575.md) hospital in the world.[^cite_note-455][^cite_note-456]

## Culture

Main article: [Culture of Haiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Haiti)

Haiti has a lasting and unique cultural identity, blending traditional French and African customs, mixed with sizable acquirements from the Spanish and Indigenous cultures.[^cite_note-Yurnet-Thomas-457]

### National holidays and festivals

Further information: [Public holidays in Haiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_holidays_in_Haiti)

The [Haitian Carnival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haitian_Carnival) has been one of the most popular carnivals in the Caribbean. In 2010, the government decided to stage the event in a different city outside Port-au-Prince every year.[^cite_note-haitilibre.com-458][^cite_note-459] The National Carnival follows the popular Jacmel Carnival, which takes place a week earlier in February or March.[^cite_note-haitilibre.com-458]

[Rara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rara_festival) is a festival celebrated before [Easter](9325.md). The festival has generated a style of [Carnival music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carnival_music).[^cite_note-460][^cite_note-461]

### Architecture



Sans-Souci Palace, National History Park, Haiti

See also: [List of World Heritage Sites in the Caribbean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_World_Heritage_Sites_in_the_Caribbean)

Monuments include the [Sans-Souci Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sans-Souci_Palace) and the [Citadelle Laferrière](2303791.md), inscribed as a [World Heritage Site](44940.md) in 1982.[^cite_note-462] Situated in the Northern [Massif du Nord](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massif_du_Nord), in the [National History Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_History_Park), the structures date from the early 19th century.[^cite_note-heritage-463] The buildings were among the first built after Haiti's independence from France.  The [Citadelle Laferrière](2303791.md), the largest fortress in the Americas, is located in northern Haiti. It was built between 1805 and 1820 and is today referred to by some Haitians as the [eighth wonder of the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eighth_wonder_of_the_world).[^cite_note-Reading_Eagle-108]

The [Institute for the Protection of National Heritage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institut_de_Sauvegarde_du_Patrimoine_National) has preserved 33 historical monuments and the historic center of Cap-Haïtien.[^cite_note-464]

[Jacmel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacmel), a colonial city that was tentatively accepted as a World Heritage Site, was extensively damaged by the 2010 earthquake.[^cite_note-heritage-463]

### Museums



*Santa María*'s anchor on display

The anchor of Christopher Columbus's largest ship, the *[Santa María](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Mar%C3%ADa_\(ship\))* rests in the [Musée du Panthéon National Haïtien](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mus%C3%A9e_du_Panth%C3%A9on_National_Ha%C3%AFtien) in Port-au-Prince.[^cite_note-465]

### Art



Swearing-in ceremony of Haitian Diaspora GwètòDe

[Haitian art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haitian_art) is distinctive, particularly through its paintings and sculptures.[^cite_note-Yurnet-Thomas-457][^cite_note-466][^cite_note-467] Brilliant colors, [naïve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Na%C3%AFve_art) perspectives, and sly humor characterize Haitian art. Frequent subjects in Haitian art include big, foods, landscapes, market activities, jungle animals, rituals, dances, and gods. As a result of a deep history and strong African ties, symbols take on great meaning within Haitian society. Many artists cluster in 'schools' of painting, such as the Cap-Haïtien school, which features depictions of daily life in the city, the Jacmel School, which reflects the steep mountains and bays of that coastal town, or the Saint-Soleil School, which is characterized by abstracted human forms and is heavily influenced by Vodou symbolism.

In the 1920s the *indigéniste* movement gained international acclaim, with its expressionist paintings inspired by Haiti's culture and African roots. Notable painters of this movement include [Hector Hyppolite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hector_Hyppolite), Philomé Oban and [Préfète Duffaut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pr%C3%A9f%C3%A8te_Duffaut).[^cite_note-Bradt36-468] Some notable artists of more recent times include [Edouard Duval-Carrié](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edouard_Duval-Carri%C3%A9), Frantz Zéphirin, Leroy Exil, [Prosper Pierre Louis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prosper_Pierre_Louis) and [Louisiane Saint Fleurant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiane_Saint_Fleurant).[^cite_note-Bradt36-468] Sculpture is also practiced in Haiti; noted artists in this form include George Liautaud and [Serge Jolimeau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serge_Jolimeau).[^cite_note-Bradt37-469]

### Music and dance

[Haitian music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Haiti) combines a wide range of influences drawn from the many people who have settled here. It reflects French, African and Spanish elements and others who have inhabited Hispaniola, and minor native Taino influences. Styles of music unique to Haitian culture include music derived from [Vodou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haitian_Vodou) ceremonial traditions, [Rara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rara_music) parading music, [Twoubadou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twoubadou) *ballads*, [mini-jazz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mini-jazz) rock bands, [Rasin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rasin) movement, [Hip hop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haitian_hip_hop) kreyòl, [méringue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C3%A9ringue),[^cite_note-470] and [compas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Compas). Youth attend parties at nightclubs called *[discos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nightlife)*, and attend *Bal* (ball, as in a formal dance).

*[Compas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Compas) (konpa)*[^cite_note-471] is a complex, ever-changing music that arose from [African rhythms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhythm_in_Sub-Saharan_Africa) and European ballroom dancing, mixed with Haiti's bourgeois culture. It is a refined music, with [méringue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C3%A9ringue) as its basic rhythm. Haiti had no recorded music until 1937 when [Jazz Guignard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jazz_Guignard) was recorded non-commercially.[^cite_note-472]

### Literature

Main article: [Haitian literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haitian_literature)

Haiti has produced poetry, novels, and plays of international recognition. The [French colonial experience](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint-Domingue) established the French language as the venue of culture and prestige, and since then it has dominated the literary circles and the literary production. However, since the 18th century there has been a sustained effort to write in [Haitian Creole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haitian_Creole). The recognition of Creole as an official language has led to an expansion of novels, poems, and plays in Creole.[^cite_note-473] In 1975, [Franketienne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franketienne) was the first to break with the French tradition in fiction with the publication of *Dezafi*, the first novel written entirely in Haitian Creole.[^cite_note-474] Other well known Haitian authors include [Jean Price-Mars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean_Price-Mars), [Jacques Roumain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacques_Roumain), [Jacques Stephen Alexis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacques_Stephen_Alexis), [Marie Vieux-Chauvet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marie_Vieux-Chauvet), Pierre Clitandre, [René Depestre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ren%C3%A9_Depestre), [Edwidge Danticat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edwidge_Danticat), [Lyonel Trouillot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyonel_Trouillot) and [Dany Laferrière](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dany_Laferri%C3%A8re).

### Folklore and mythology

Main article: [Haitian mythology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haitian_mythology)

Haiti is known for its folklore traditions.[^cite_note-475] Much of this is rooted in [Haitian Vodou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haitian_Vodou) tradition. Belief in [zombies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zombies) is also common.[^cite_note-Bradt35-476] Other folkloric creatures include the [lougarou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soucouyant).[^cite_note-Bradt35-476]

### Cinema

Main article: [Cinema of Haiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_Haiti)

| icon | This section **does not [cite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Citing_sources) any [sources](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Verifiability)**. Please help [improve this section](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:EditPage/Haiti) by [adding citations to reliable sources](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Referencing_for_beginners). Unsourced material may be challenged and [removed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Verifiability#Burden_of_evidence). *(January 2026)*_&#x20;([Learn how and when to remove this message](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Maintenance_template_removal))_ |
| --- | --- |

Haiti has a small though growing cinema industry. Well-known directors working primarily in documentary film-making include [Raoul Peck](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raoul_Peck) and [Arnold Antonin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arnold_Antonin). Directors producing fictional films include [Patricia Benoît](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patricia_Benoit_\(director\)), Wilkenson Bruna and Richard Senecal.

### Cuisine

Main article: [Haitian cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haitian_cuisine)

Haiti is famous for its [creole cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Creole_cuisine) (related to [Cajun cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cajun_cuisine)), and its [soup joumou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soup_joumou).[^cite_note-477]

### Sports

Main article: [Culture of Haiti § Sports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Haiti#Sports)



[Haiti national football team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haiti_national_football_team) training in Port-au-Prince, 2004

[Football](10568.md) (soccer) is the most popular sport in Haiti with hundreds of small clubs competing at the local level. Basketball and baseball are growing in popularity.[^cite_note-478][^cite_note-sport-479] [Stade Sylvio Cator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stade_Sylvio_Cator) is the [multi-purpose stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multi-purpose_stadium) in Port-au-Prince, currently used mostly for [association football](10568.md) matches. In [1974](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1974_FIFA_World_Cup), the [Haiti national football team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haiti_national_football_team) were only the second [Caribbean](18956035.md) team to make the [World Cup](11370.md), returned to the tournament in [2026](36581929.md) after 52 years of absence. The national team won the [2007 Caribbean Nations Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007_Caribbean_Nations_Cup).[^cite_note-480] The [women's team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haiti_women's_national_football_team) also made the [FIFA World Cup](244862.md) in [2023.](45271353.md)[^cite_note-481]

Haiti has participated in the [Olympic Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haiti_at_the_Olympics) since the year 1900 and won a number of medals. Haitian footballer [Joe Gaetjens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joe_Gaetjens) played for the [United States national team](582488.md) in the [1950 FIFA World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1950_FIFA_World_Cup), scoring the winning goal in the 1–0 upset of [England](9904.md).[^cite_note-482]

[^cite_note-12]: &#x20;[French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_language): *République d'Haïti*; [Haitian Creole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haitian_Creole_language): *Repiblik Ayiti*<sup>[\[10\]](#cite_note-11)</sup>

[^cite_note-Hayti-18]: &#x20;The nation was officially founded as *Hayti* in its Declaration of Independence and early prints,<sup>[\[11\]](#cite_note-auto5-13)</sup><sup>[\[12\]](#cite_note-auto3-14)</sup> constitutions,<sup>[\[13\]](#cite_note-auto2-15)</sup> and imperial declarations.<sup>[\[14\]](#cite_note-auto4-16)</sup> Published writings of 1802–1919 in the United States commonly used the name *Hayti* (e.g. *The Blue Book of Hayti* (1919), a book with official standing in Haiti). By 1873 *Haiti* was common among titles of US published books as well as in US congressional publications. In all of [Frederick Douglass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frederick_Douglass)' publications after 1890, he used *Haiti*. As late as 1949, the name *Hayti* continued to be used in books published in England (e.g. *Hayti: 145 Years of Independence—The Bi-Centenary of Port-au-Prince* published in London, England in 1949) but by 1950, usage in England had shifted to *Haiti*.<sup>[\[15\]](#cite_note-auto1-17)</sup>

[^cite_note-44]: &#x20;The Taínos may have used *Bohío* as another name for the island.<sup>[\[38\]](#cite_note-41)</sup><sup>[\[39\]](#cite_note-42)</sup><sup>[\[40\]](#cite_note-43)</sup>

French losses from 1830–51 were 3,336 killed in battle and 92,329 died of wounds or from all other causes. Between 1830 and 1870, 411 French officers were killed and 1,360 were wounded. The toll for the ranks was an estimated 10,000 killed and 35,000 wounded in all French colonial campaigns. A few thousand from this number died in Mexico or Indochina, but the great bulk met their deaths in Algeria. Disease took an even greater toll. One estimate puts total French and Foreign Legion deaths from battle and disease for the entire century at 110,000.
